" asked his wife. "Well, I have just been reading a lot of stories about English women, and there wasn't one of them who had a thing to wear." ABOVE PROOF.—A Congo explorer was busy cleaning his weapons with vi-riol. Our explorer jumped up, expecting to see the fellow die in frightful agonies. But his sable majesty merely rubbed his stomach with evident satisfaction and said i—" What! you keep such nice things in your bag without offering them to your friend !" THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, PAUGUST 31. 1895. LADIES' LETTER.

MT DEAREST NELLIE, Although it is too near the end of the summer to invest in expensive gowns of thin texture, yet many people at this time of the year find it neces- sary either to purchase a new dress which will serve for early autumn wear, or to re-trim and generally furbish up a spring frock for the same purpose. For the former nothing is better than a light-weight serge in a rather bright shade of navy blue, which can be made up in a variety of ways. A dress which may be taken as a good example of what is considered fashionable has the usual plain bell-skirt, and a bodice with a round collar at the back and revers to the waist in front of cream flannel, striped with rather close-set horizontal rows of narrow blue braid. The full vest is of cream and blue figured surah, and the belt and plain collar - band are of blue velvet, but the long tight cuffs are of braided flannel. This dress, worn with a coarse cream straw sailor hat of the new bell-crowned shape, trimmed with big rosettes of cream ribbon and wings of the same colour would make a smart costume for a fashionable seaside resort, while if, on the other hand, a more useful type of gown is wanted, the white flannel might be replaced by tan cloth. The vest could be of white washing silk tacked in so as to be easily removable' and a navy straw hat might finish +lie costume. Another and

rather more novel way making up a serge gown is to arrange the front of the bodice in a very wide box pleat, tapering just a little to the waist, a row of rather large flat oxydised silver buttons being placed down the edges of this pleat. The skirt has simulated fastenings on the hips, with three but- tons sewn down a shallow flap of the material, and the bodice is finished by a broad belt of navy rib- bon fastening with a silver clasp. A very pretty dreqs I saw at a well-known watering-place last week had a bodice cut in the Eton style, but the fronts, instead of being left loose, were caught together at the waist, or a shade below, with a silver clasp, above which the jacket was turned back with wide revers forming a round collar at the back of black silk covered with cream lace. The vest, slightly full, was of white silk, and the gauntlet cuffs matched the revers. One of the fashionable Panama hats, trimmed with rosettes of white chiffon and white plumes, MA worn with this gown.

There are two distinct classes in millinery for the present sea;on. First we have the sailor and boat hat shapes, which are simply trimmed with a ribbon band, and flat bow on one side; these hats are made of Panama, coarse, fine, and fancy straws, in black, navy, white, and burnt tints. Dressy hats for the afternoon are chiefly trimmed with chiffons, flowers and feathers being introduced together with loops of coloured silk ribbons. A pretty hat, for example, with a low crown and fiat brim, in a burnt shade of straw, has the crown oovered with pale yellow chiffon, gathered so that it sets full and rather high; an upstanding spray of crimson roses is placed on one side of it, and three small rosettes of the chiffon on the other, with a few more crimson roses. Another hat in a wide sailor shape of coarse white straw has a flounce of pleated white chiffon laid upon the brim from the base of the crown; the front is trimmed with a full wide bow of black and white striped glac4 silk. A large spray of pink roses is placed at the back of the crown, arranged so that some of the flowers set upwards, and some fall under the brim on to the hair. Small toque-shaped hats are considered smart enough to take the place of bonnets, and are more worn than the latter in the country. They are trimmed with full ribbon bows placed at the back of the crown, and symmetrical posies of flowers or rosettes of chiffon on either side of the front. A fashionable hat of the second

class I mentioned has a brim of pleated black lace, and the crown of coarse pink straw. There are loops of pink and yellow shot glacd ribbon caught with paste buckles in the front, and sprays of pink and yellow roses.

Your loving JEN Nu.

Five lives were lost off Skegness, on the Lincc,lnshire coast, On Sunday evening, by the capsizlng of a pleasure boat on a cruise from Flunstanton, on the Norfolk side of The Wash, with sixteen pas.sengers and crew. The accident was due to the yawl tieing caught in a sudden squall. Eleven of the occupants were, by the coolness and courage of the skipper, got into a small boat which was towed by the yawl, and they were afterwards picked up by a trawler and landed at Boston.

The Queen once wrote some verses which were for- warded to a publisher under a nom de plume, and were promptly returned " with thanks." A new occupation for a woman is that of superintend- ent of weddings. A professional lady of this sort who ' makes a success of the calling is installed in or near the house of the bride prospective some little time be fore the ceremony. She selects the trousseau, advises what is latest and finest in underwear, buys the material, and designs and makes or superintends the making of the gowns. She is au fait in stockings, boots, gloves, laces, and handkerchiefs. She sees to the millinery and jackets and wraps. She dresses the bride's mother and the younger sisters, if avy. She dictates to the bridesmaids and is the fairy godmother who thinks of everything and lets the engaged couple enjoy themselves with unanxious mind. The family, who have found her valuable,recom- mend her to another, and she soon obtains quite a clientele.

An elderly spinster at Vienna wrote to the Town Coun- cil that be had placed at their disposal £5,000 for the benefit of one of the hospitals. Her offer, needless to say, was accepted with gratitude,and all the newspapers brought out aiticles praising Mlle. 11--- for her libera- lity. The money was duly deposited at the bank ; but when the donees went to clam it they found it had been withdrawn the previous day. They wrote at once te Mlle. R—for an explanation ; but, as all the letters remained unanswered ,one of the Town Councillors waited on the aged lady. With many blushes, and covered with confusion, she informed the visitor that her generosity had brought her luck,as on the very morning she parted with the money she had an offer of marriage. Naturally, she had at once run to the bank,and withdrawn toe five thousand pounds.

Sleeping on the Same Side. It is not wise to put a child to sleep always on the same side, even though the habit may seem to decrease restlessness. Constantly lying on one side will make a difference in the size of the limbs upon that side, and will even cause that side of the face to remain smaller than the other. Children will also draw up one leg in their sleep. This, too, becomes a fixed habit, and by the time the child has learned to walk,a difference in the length of the two lower limbs will he noticed, a misfor- tune which might have been avoided had the mother been careful to watch the habits of the sleeping baby.

Apple Diet for the Skin. A ripe, raw apple is one of the easiest foods for the stomach to deal with, the whole process of digestion only consuming eighty-five minutes. The rustic acid of ripe apples' cooked or raw, helps to digest meat arid stimulate the liver and neutralise those noxious matters which, unless elminated, produce skin eruptions. Apples are not as satisfying as potatoes, because of their deli- cate elements, but eaten with meat, in place of tubers, they are a golden food. The salts and wine sweeten the stomach, the phosphorus is thought to be a nerve buitder, and women of all ages, since Eve's days, like to believe that the " food of the gods imparted its deli- cate white to the flesh. Why not ? Beef-eaters and wine-drinkers are red.

How to Make the Best of Life. The man or woman, says Sir Benjamin Ward Richard- son, who trains himself in the best bodily health makes the best of life. Bodily welfare is important, not for itself only, but because the health of the mind so largely depends en the health of the body. A good engine out- live many of Its masters because they attend to it more carefully than they attend to their own bodies. The usual relations of the age of maturity to length of life, indicating a ratio of one to five, suggest that a man tak- ing twenty-one years to mature should live one hundred and five years. The fact that such life is exceptionally attained shows its possibility ; and it is owing to errors that it is not more widely attained in the human species.

Coffeeism,

Some weeks before his death, the late Professor Char- cot was says " Science Siftings," in attendance upon a family composed of the father, mother and six children, who had become the victims of an uncontrollable mental irritability upon the least provocation. Hardly a meal passed at the family board without an explosion. Upon the least pretext the father became furious, the mother scolded, and toe children would give way to hysterical crying. The family were all hypochondriacal. The , strangest part of the history consisted in the fact that domestics employed and residing in the family would soon become troubled in the same way and join in the pandemonium. In the middle ages, this house would have been considered haunted, and somebody would have been burned or hung and quartered as having enchanted the premises This was the nineteenth century, however, and as demons, goblins, fairies and vampires are no longer in fact, Charcot looked into the hygiene of the locality for a solution of the difficulties. On investigation he found that the father was a manufacturer and dealer in coffee; the roasting, grinding and packing, as well as the menu- fActuri ng of the essence and of the extract of coffee, being carried on in the lower floors of the premiseQ. In the apartments above the odour of coffee pervaded every nook; the furniture and clothing smelt strongly of coffee. The inmates were suffering from chronic coffee- ism. A few weeks' residence in the purer air of thti sea- shore and change of habitation soon brought about a change for the better.

A SMART BUSINESS MAN.

Mr. TI. Cuthbert, of 10, Coply-street, Everton, Liver- pool, described by a reporter as "a smart business man," has been interviewed in regard to a fortunate occurrence in his family. In April,lB94, Mrs. Cuthbert suffered severe illness, b. coming partially incapacitated for work. She was treated by doctors, but grew worse, and in November h.-1:i to give up work entirely. She sought the aid of another medical man. Up to nine weeks ago he regu- lativ attended her, but she nevertheless grew worse. Daring all these months khe grew thus almost to a shldow, and constantly, night and day, suffered from two distinct pains—one situated in the head, the other I a the gide. She was excessively nervous, and suffered all the time from such severe lassitude that to use from a chair was a task she began to shrina from. She lost all hope of living. A friend. recommended her to try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. She did so simply as an experiment, believing that nothing could cure her. But before she had taken the first package of pills she was recovering. " I felt," she said, " that I was re- covering—as though there was something at work in my constitution building me up. I can't say anything else except that I am in ray fifth box, and I have been back to work for three weeks, and, as you see, I am perfectly strong again. MEN AS MACHINES.

Writing in the" North American Review" Mr. Mul- hall, the atatiatician,gives some curious facts reflecting the working power of various races.. Since 1840 the working power of the individual in the United States has been almost doubled through useful inventions. The working power, measured in foot-tons, is in the States 1,940 tons daily, while the working power of an indivi- dual in Great Britain is 1,470 tons. The working power of a single person in America is twice that of a German nr a Frenchman. more than three times that of an Aus- trian, and five times that of an Italian. In 1890 the United States produced 350 bushels of grain and 1,2301 b. ef meat per hand employed, while each hsnd employed in similar tasks in the United Kingdom produced only 119 bushels of grain and 1,0901 b. of meat. In France this production amounted to only 98 bushels of grain and 3501 b. of meat ; while in Germany. Austria, and Italy it was still less. As Mr. Mulhall puts it :—An or- dinary farm hand in the United States raised as much grain as three in England, four in France, five in Ger- many, or six in Austria,which shows what an enormous waste of labour occurs in Europe because farmers are not possessed of the same mechanical appliances as in the United States.

GLENALPINE'S OATH;

OR, THE MARK OF THE BLACK CROSS. -- By SYLVANUS COBB, 411. •

CHAPTER E.

GLEAMS OF TRUE FRIENDSHIP. Overlooking the beautiful Esplanade of Chow- ringhee was the dwelling which Sir Jasper Wallace occupied when in Calcutta. Upon his arrival he found the various apartments ready for his recep- tion, and, as the weather was fine, his niece was delighted with the situation. Arthur Rainsford, by the old soldier's express command, was installed as a member of the family. "I will have it so," he said, when Arthur would have demurred. "I demand it as a favour to my- self. You have no other home in India, and until you join your regiment you shall make your home with me. If you would rebel, you can do so; but I fancy that the result would fall more heavily upon you than upon myself." Not a word was said concerning Mary, and yet our hero was sure that Sir Jasper had her in his mind while was speaking. One thing gave the youth encouragement: He discovered that the son of Colonel Tom Rainford came to Calcutta with a sure passport to favour, and he felt sure that if he did not win and retain that favour it must be his own fault. It was his father who had, in Burbank's time, held a pass of the Mahratta, single-handed, against a score of murderous Bengalese for more than an hour. It was Tom Rainsford, too, who had brought Sir Phillip Harlowe off the field at Nagore, cutting down three savage warriors who would have blocked up his way, even while he upheld the drooping form of his commander. Arthur was proud to claim his sonship of such a father, and he resolved that he would uphold the honour of the name so far as he could. Surely our hero had plenty of incentive to high and honourable action, and he did what every truly earnest and devout man does who courts success in honourable enterpi ise—he looked to Heaven, and prayed for strength and guidance. "Well, my boy," said Sir Jasper, tapping Arthur upon the shoulder, on the third evening after their snivel in Calcutta, "I have good news for you. Our people are only too glad to accommodate us. They not anly recognise you under the commission I gave you from Bristol, but they give you the commission from the day of the battle. And mark you, my dear boy, they don't do this particularly as a favour to me. They have seen Captain Harlow, and he has assured them that they owe their ship to you. It's all right; so give me the hand of CAPTAIN ARTHUR RAINSFORD." "But," said our hero, as soon as he had suffi- ciently recovered from his delicious surprise, "these commissions must be bought." "The lieutenant's commission has already been sold again." "But the last commission. 1 cannot—" " Tut i tut ! You mean that you feel delicate about accepting the gift from me ?" "Not exactly that, Sir Jasper. lam willing to receive anything in reason; but that you should pay out money for me—that you should—" Hold on 1" interrupted the general, with a gratified smile. "I am happy to be able to set your mind entirely at rest in that direction. I had intended to purchase the commission for you, but the agent of the Company, acting from informa- tion received from Harlow, has anticipated me. Know, my boy, that you receive your commission from the hands of the Company itself, not as a gift, but in recognition of valuable services ren- dered. Are you satisfied ?" Arthur was more than satisfied—he was happily pleased; and he expressed his gratitude in a modest and becoming manner. "And now to business," pusued Wallace. "Your regiment is at Agra and you need not join it for two months; but you are not exactly on leave. lam on duty and you have been detailed to serve on my staff. lam going to Patna on business and you will accompany me; that is, if you would like. But if you prefer to join your regiment at once you can do so." Arthur had no thought of shirking duty, but he much preferred accompanying Sir Jasper as aide- de-camp to the other service. When he joined his regiment he must be far away from Mary, but while he served upon Sir Jasper's staff the rapturous bond was not severed. And he sig- nified to his friend that he would go with him to Patna. " You need have no fear," said the baronet, " that our duties are to be entirely pastime. You may have heard that there is trouble among the Nabobs. The great Sahibs are by the ears. The President of Madras is in a fit of the doldrums, and he of Bombay sustains him, and together they are inclined to give the governor-general trouble." "I have heard something of it, Sir Jasper. Will we have to go to Madras?" "Not at present. The governor-general informs me that a commission is coming from the Home Office. We shall await that arrival." As the mission to Patna was simply one of busi- ness, and as the state of the country through the jungles of the flanges was rather unsettled, It Was deemed best that Mary should remain in Calcutta. She was left in charge of Colonel Tabor, an officer attached to the Bengal Governmbut and a warm personal friend of Sir Jasper's. It was beneath the Colonel's roof that Sir Jasper had made his home, he having advanced money towards its purchase and still holding an interest in the estate. Arthur, in modest and serviceable uniform be- fitting his rank and armed and equipped for his journey, held Mary's hand, ready to take his leave. "The future give promise, darling, and I am hopeful," be said, buoyantly. "Life is before us, Arthur," the maiden returned, looking up with a solemn smile "and God grant that no evil genius be permitted to obstruct our bright pathway." "We will have no fears, Mary. With our hearts pure and our resolves good we will trust in God." He kissed her upon the lips, and with a fer- vent God's blessing he went away upon hie mis- sion.

Within a week after the departure of Sir Jasper for Patna another arrival from England was sig- nalled from Diamond Point, and in due time the new-comer had reached the city, and among them were Lord Brichmear and his family. His lord- ship at once waited upon the governor-general and was informed that his services would not be required away from Calcutta until General Sir Jasper Wal- lace should return from Patna. "Bless the dear old fellow I So he is out here still 2" cried Birchmear. He was informed that Sir Jasper had but just

come out. " How ? Just come out ?" "Yes. He came in the Golden Fleece from Bristol." " Ah ! what a season of joy I have lost !" said Sir John, regretfully. "Had I known that Wal- lace was to have sailed in that ship, I would have given a year's service for the blessing of his com- pany. He and I commenced our ladla life together under Clive." "You have nothing to regret, my lord," said the governor, "your lucky star was in the ascen- dant." " How so?" "Not all who started from Old England on board the Gulden Fleece lived to reach India. She met with a terrible shock on the way." And thereupon the governor-general told the Story of the battle with the pirate, as he bad heard it from Sir Jasper's own lips. l;less me t" ejaculated Birchmear. "The old soldier had a narrow chance of it. Was he woo riled ?" "No,—and yet he was nearer to his death than ever , efore. Aye, he who has stood face to face with the grim spectre so many times, declares that never before did he feel the cold grip upon him." "I bless the chance that saved him, be it what It may," pronounced Lord Birchmear, fervently. "Was it a—" "It was a hero," said the governor, as Sir John hesitated for a word. "And, my dear old friend, as you are likely to meet this prodigy, if you ever meet again with Sir Jasper, I will tell you about it. Our hero was a young man who had taken passage on board the ship. The pirates attacked full two to one, and when they had boarded the Indiaman this same youth by a sort of electric power which sat upon him of its own accord, be- came the leader of the resisting force. He did not assume command, but his deeds gave him the position. According to Captain Harlow's account, and also the account of others, he performed pro- digies of valour; and it may be no stretch to say that his single arm decided the victory. He had struck down the pirate chieftain after a hand-lic.han& conflict. and was being sorely

pressed by those who won. s when he saw Sir Jasper on the oppojlie side the deck, in the hands of t‘,"° stout pirates,one of whom drew him backward overa gun, while the other made ready to plunge a ,Torn into his. bosom. Like a thunderbolt our heroin. oke through the mur- the cordon, and was in time to smite hot. derous villains to the death ere they „,k1 do the old soldier harm." "By Jove!" exclaimed Birchmear, who had listened with deepest interest, "how it warms on. a blood to hear of such deeds. I must certain])' see this young fellow. What is he doing here in India?" "He is now with Sir Jasper as an aide. We have given him a captain's commission." "And I trust he is a likely fellow, and of good family." "As to the first proposition, my lord, I can safely assure you that a. likelier fellow does not exist. He a gentleman of the very first water, as modest as he is brave, and as true and manly as he is hand- some—which is saying much. Touching his family he come, of a stock which we in India have learned to set among the very highest in true value. Don't you remember Tom Rainsford, the Lion of the Mar hratta, as we used to call him r "Colonel Itainaford 1" said Birchmear, with a start. "Aye—Colonel Tom. He headed the charge of Wallace's Division at Hajepoor, and fell after he had won the victory." "I remember him," answered the old nobleman, trembling perceptibly. "But what of him "This young hero of whom we speak is his son." "The son of Tom Itainsford I Is it Arthur ?" "Yes. But—bless me I—l had entirely forgotten. You married Tom's widow, Sir John !" Birchmear put forth his hand, and sank into a chair. "My lord! My dear Sir John 1" "Hush !" whispered the baron, arising again to his feet. "Allis well with me. It was but a passing pang. I know the youth. You say he is now with Sir Jasper ?" " Yes ; and the old general loves him as though he were of his own flesh and blood." "Then I shall see him. And now, my lord, you may crack a bottle of wine." They adjourned to a small parlour in the rear of the office, where they talked of lighter matters over a bottle of old Burgundy. At a later hour Lord Birchmear was alone in his own apartment. He had not stopped to see his wife' and he had refused to drive down the river with Harold agreeably to an arrangement which had been made in the morning. His stepson had asked him why he could not go, and he had pleaded business. He was pacing up and down the room, slowly and thoughtfully, when Peter Brunn came in. Peter had been the old porter and lodge. keeper at Birchmear' tied to his post for many years, and his master had taken him along to India, partly out of compassion, and partly In considera- tion of the belief that Peter was honest and reliable. But Lady Birchmear and Harold had opposed the move; they had declared that Peter Brun was too old and stupid for service in India, and they doubted if he would survive the voyage; but Sir John had been determined. Not only had Peter been anxious to see something of the world before he died, but the baron had felt that in the person of his old lodge-keeper he would be sure to possess a faithful and devoted friend. It may be that he had, not much confidence in the reliability of the servants whom his lady proposed to take. "My lord," said Peter, standing just over the threshold, "I came to see if I could be spared after dinner. A friend wishes me to go with him and see the sights of the Black Town," "You can go Peter." The old • servitor had stepped back over the threshold when his master called for him to return. "Come in, Peter, and shut the door." The man stepped back into the room and closed the door behind him. Lord Birchmear had never yet opened his lips upon family affairs in the presence of his servants; much less had he ever taken a menial into his confidence upon such matters; but he was growing old, and the cordon of strict social proprieties which had hedged him about was giving way before the needs of the hour. Ile had come to feel that Lady Birchmear was not entirely devoted to his interest—or, a least, that she was not in a mood to surrender self in the way of such devotion. Touching Harold he had grave doubts. But touching the entire and unselfish devotion of Peter un he had no doubts whatever. And yet he had not thought of trusting Peter very far. He only meant to sound him—to gain intelligence from him without exposing his own mind. He did not deem that the old lodge-keeper had wit enough to read the thoughts of a master, especially when those thoughts should be carefully kept under the surface. "Peter," said his lordship, in a kindly, confiden- tial manner—a manner in which he was not wont to address his servants—"'you remember Arthur Rainsford ?" "Rainsford, my lord!" " So we must call him now." "If you mean the young master, my lord, I do certainly remember him." "And you have known of all his in-goings and out-comings at Birchmear "No one knew more of him at Birchmear, my lord, than I did," replied Peter, with much sur- prise; for never before had his lordship deigned to speak with him upon a matter so nearly con- nected with the private affairs of the family. "And," pursued Sir John, "I have no doubt that you learned to regard him as a graceless rascal 7" Peter Brun was nonplussed. He would not out- rage his own sense of justice even to please his master, and he was not quite sure that it would be Safe to speak his mind freely. "You do not answer me, Peter." "Indeed, my lord, I don't know what answer can make. You can judge best for yourself how Mr. Arthur stood at Birchmear. Your heard all the reports." "Aye, Peter, I heard all the reports; but I did not witness all the facts. You had better oppor- tunities of seeing his thousand-and-one shortcom- ings." This implication, so broad and so unfounded, and coming, as Peter knew it must, from information whispered by the boy's enemies, ivoused his indig- nation and his tongue was loosened. "My lord," he said, with a flush upon his time- worn face and a keen snap of the clear grey eyes, "I didn't see those shortcomings. If you turn me out from your doors for it I will speak the tiuth. Mr. Arthur was a wild and venturesome boy, and I know he had little fear of consequences when his wayward spirit led him towards difficulties; but there's a difference between the pranks and the frolics that come of a gay and happy heart, full of life and adventure, and those that come of an evil disposition. I don't befieve the boy ever did an evil thing in his life, really thinking and knowing that evil was to conic. Pardon me, my lord, but I think you misjudged the lad." "Misjudged him, Peter? Have you forgotten his course at the manor ?" "No, air, I haven't forgotten it; but perhaps I don't judge him in the Salllo light that you do." "You You would overlook his faults, Peter 7" "Just the same, sir, as I would overlook the frolicking disposition of a young colt which I saw had in him the material and the mettle for a magnificent racer or a grand and reliable war- horse." "I declare, Peter, you are inclined to look leniently upon the young man's faults." The old servitor saw no sign of offence in his master's look, so lie frankly answered : "I would never repine over what can be mended, my lord. I can forgive one whom I love, and who never did me harm. "Peter, I do not think the other servants would agree with you." "If you mean those who were at Birchmear in the old times, my lord, I can speak for every one. Mr. Arthur was their joy, sir. The house was always brighter and warmer when he was in it; and even out in the kennels and in the stables there was new life. The dogs and the horses loved him. But if you speak of those that have come in later times, I can't answer for them, sir. I never have gained their confidence." "That will do, Peter. You can go." "You are not offended, my lord ?' No, no. If there in harm in your speaking the honest truth, the fault must be with me who set you to the task. No Peter, lam not offended." The servitor withdrew, and Sir John was left once more alone. "I think," he said, to himself, as he stood with his hands folded before him, "that my old love for the boy was not misplaced. I wish he had not taken that money! I could have forgiven every- thing else, Two thousand pounds was not much for me to lose; but it was too much for him to— rritu.l If he had been of a stamp truly noble, he would have starved before he would have sobbed his old step-father, 01 I wish he had not dane THAT 1 11.1•41 k 1.140 obgr._with Me herd bowel s

There may be anothe.r_ side to the story—another side brighter than thli aoltioh now we see. God grant it 1" (To be continued). _

"CAPITAL" CHAT

I do not know whether it is a result of an order by the late Liberal or by the Unionist Government that the work in some of the dockyards is now be- ing pushed on with accelerated speed; but both at Portsmouth and Chatham orders have been re- ceived to have the Majestic and Magnificent,which are being built in these dockyards respectively, completed twelve months earlier than was origin- ally intended. If this energy is continued both vessels will be'commissioned in six or eight months' time. In both dockyards the overtime at present is about four hours a day, and probably a Thousand men in each yard are working overtime. At Devon. port also torpedoes are being turned out night and day without stopping. If all this speed ie the re- sult of orders by the late Government, the present Government, with their Imperial ideas, are not likely to reduce it. ' They are much more likely to go upon the tack of bettering the example set them by their predecessors.

The great Civil Service Association, a large trad- ing concern and universal provider, is on the eve of an important development which will bring it still further into direct competition with the ordinary tradesman. The Association was originally formed as a co-operative institution for civil servants, and to that extent it was absolutely unassailable. But, if not at the beginning, then very ear:y in its career, it admitted to its benefits the friends or acquaintances of civil servants, there being a limit fixed to the number of outsiders so benefited. These outsiders paid fis. yearly, which was afterwards reduced to 2s. 6c1., for the privilege they enjoyed. For many years dividends were not allowed to be distributed among the original civil servant share- holders; but of late years enormous dividends have been paid in which the outside ticket-holders had no share. The institution thus became a mere traders' establishment, trading for profit. The further development which is contemplated is an increase in the number of outsiders and the admis- sion of the whole body of outsiders to many of the privileges of shareholders. One of the privileges denied to them is, of course, a share in the divid- end. This new evolutionary movement will lead I once more to widespread dissatisfaction among the tradesmen in and around London, who have often risen in indignant protest against this competition, and invariably without the slightest effect. Every year sees an enormous development of the Associa- tion's business.

Sir Matthew W.Ridley will soon be known as the Minister of All the Promises. Not a day passes without the new Home Secretary undertaking in the most agreeable manner to give his "careful con- sideration" to All sorts of subjects during the re- cess. The Ministetialists do not take a very san- guine view either of the Parliamentary or the ad- ministrative capacity of their new Home Secretary, who so far has only succeeded in confusing serious issues, and attaching undue importance to minor things.

Homburg, where the Prince of Wales has gone to drink the waters, is one of the most fairy-like of the German watering places. It was formerly in the Duchy of Nassau, and at that time had famous gam- ing-tables, but since its annexation to Prussia these have become things of the past. Homburg is full of nothing but smart people, and the place is so charm- ing, and all the folks so well dressed, that you feel that if a collection were made for the poor there would be no one to receive it. As few people open their eyes before six, those at Homburg ought to be the most amiable and happy in the world, for the delightful strains of a band begin at that bour,play- ing the morning hymn, a soft, exquisite melody, with wtich the day is always opened.. The band continues to play while the patients take their morning draught, walking round and round the grass plats to settle it,

Lying in a jewellery establishment in New Bond-street is a remarkable diamond which has recently been procured from the famous De Beers Mines, in South Africa. Besides being of abnormal size, the diamond is curious, because its round ow-- line is found to resemble a human face in profile. Well-proportioned features are clearly chiselled out, nose, mouth, and chin being all very plainly de- fined. Hardly had the outline of a human face been discovered, than it was suggested that in cer- tain lights it bore a resemblance to Lord Salisbury. The diamond was then, by the Unionist leader's per- mission. called the " Salisbury" diamond. Expert evidence places it beyond all doubt that the outline of the diamond is the work of nature, and that the hand of man or men has had no part in producing the supposed likeness.

When Mr. Yerburah put his question in the House of Commons to the Home Secretary concerning the ways of some English usurers, be had in his pocket and was ready to produce, a promissory note given to a Welsh farmer by a Birmingham money lender which note was headed, " Wellington-street, Glas- gow." The effect of this arrangement was to enable the money lender to proceed under Scottish law against his debtor, and to levy distress upon him without any notice beyond the insertion of an ad- vertisement in a Scottish newspaper. Many far- mers in Hereford and Worcester have, it has come to Mr. Yerburgh's knowledge, been subject to this kind of practice.

Lord Rosebery has evidently won the affection of several tradesmen in the Avenue named after him. There is in this Islington thoroughfare a Rosebery Coffee nouse, a Rosebery if air-dressing Saloon, a Rosebery Dairy, and other instances of the popular- ity of the ex• Premier.

One of the last acts of the late Government was the rejection of a private Bill for the carrying out of a larze scheme. which some years ago received the sanction of Parliament,for the widening of Par- liament-st. Mr. Herbert Gladstone stated that it was the intention of the Liberal Government to ac- quire the land between the Foreign Office buildings and Westminster Abbey. partly with the object of making a noble approach to the Houses of Parlia- ment and the Abbey,and of erecting POMO extensive Government edifices. The dissolution of course brought the scheme to an end. It has,hoYvever,been taken up by the present Government, who will in- troduce during this session a non-contentious mea- sure. Late on Monday night a resolution was agreed to authorising the issue out of the Consolidated Fund of such sums as will be required for the pur. chase of the site. In due course,therefore,the Bill, which has already been drafted, will be introduced, and it will be passed without difficulty.

Mr. George Curzon, repl:c ing a few days ago to a question in the House of Commons, said that her Majesty's Government had no knowledge of the treaty concluied between France and China in re- ference to the cession to France of territory which we conditionally transferred to China two years ago. Of course he meant that the Government bad no official knowledge of it, for he was able to state that the treaty had not yet been ratified. That a knowledge of its contents had, through some trnst- worthy c'.3annel, reached Lord Salisbury, is now clear,as his lordship has taken very energetic ac- tion. He has caused it to be made known at Pekin that if any cession is intended this country will resume possession of the territory in question. The Chinese excuse, i believe, is that the cession to France was an accidental oversight of the pro vi- tions of the treaty with England. There will be no trouble, therefore, from that quarter. France also will have to back out, of its claims, though it may wait for M. Pavie's arrival from Burmah before surrendering them. There is no alternative, so long as fiord Salisbury holds by his present atti- tude. The effect of the Prime Minister's action is to shut France off from the Upper Mekong and from a large portion of the Chinese frontier.

The expenses incurred by Mr. Keir Hardie in the recent election in South West Ham are returned as amounting to £.249 ss. 4d., while those of Major Banes. M.P., his successful opponent, came to £642 12s. 3d. Each candidate paid £125 13s. 2d., as the returning officer's charges, the personal ex- penses of Mr. Keir Hardie being £lO, and those of Major Banes 1,53 ss. A peculiarity in Mr. Keir Hardie's return is the absence of expenses for agents, clerks or messengers, these personal set.: vices being rendered gratuitously. The Rev. T. Warren, who acted as election agent. says he received £175 from Mr. Keir Hardie and £7B 2s. id. from "other persons." In North West [lam the ex- penses of Mr. Froest Gray. M.P.. were £792 lld and those of Mr. Archibald Grove, £714 4s.

THE E XAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 31 1895.

WARRINGTON FLORAL AND HORTICULTURAL SOCIETY.

ANNUAL 5110 W.

The second annual show in connection with the War- rington Floral and Horticultural Society was held in the Warrington Gymnasium on Wednesday and Thursday last week. The show was a great improvement upon that of last year, and there were so many entries that the room was inconveniently crowded, so much so that if there is a corresponding increasenext year,a much larger place will be required. The exhibits were arranged with excellent effect, and along the centre of the room, acting as a di- viding line was a number of foliage plants supplied by the Warrington Corporation, and one interesting speci- men was an acacia grown by Mr. Parker, the head gardener at the park. To the right of the entrance were the exhibits ot Messrs. Dickson, of Chester, and among these were some splendid specimens et cut bloom, and near by were the exhibits of Mr. John Rylands, Mr. R. A. Naylor, and Mrs. Beamont, which consisted chiefly of plants and flowers. Mr. R. Garnett also had a number of interesting speci- mens of foreign plants. Conspicuous among these WRS a banana tree with its large leaves. fie also exhibited three Egyptian luffas, a cotton plant, and a stephanotis. These were rare plants and provoked a great amount of interest. The other exhibitors in the centre of the room also had their plants, etc., arranged in a most tasteful manner. Along either side of the ream were the exhibits 1 of fruit and vegetables, and near the door to the left was a I,r2e hoo7h covered with apples, supplied by Mr. Joseph Whitfield, of Lower Walton. At the fax end of , the room was a stall containing a large quantity of fruit, flowers, plants and vegetables, from Mrs. Massey, of Acton Grange. The stall was in carge of Mr. Birch,and was laden with articles, and there was a large quantity of honey from the apiary at Acton Grange. An interest- ing exhibit on this stall was supplied by Mr. Fletcher, of Grappenhall. It consisted of a cocoanut palm growing out of a cocoanut, and the large palm leaves had already shot out. Mr. Fletcher also exhibited on the same stall,a plate of American raspberry. In the small room behind were the exhibits of farm produce. The whole exhibition was of a high order, and great credit is due to Mr. Ryan and the committee for their efforts to make the show a success. To show the large increase in some of the departthents" we give the list of entries for this year and last. 1894 1895 increase Class I. Plants Fruits Flowers Vegetables Class U. Plants Cut bloom Fruit 7 6 —1 Vegetables Class 111. Plants 9 13 4 Flowers Vegetables ..... Class IV. Vegetables Class V. Vegetables ... 7 19 12

353 749 396

There was a large gathering at the time for the open- ing on Wednesday, and among those present were the Mayor and Mayoress (Councillor James and Mrs. Fair- (dough), Aldermen Bolton, Wright, and Hutchinson, Councillors Smethurst, Shaw Green, Cannel!, Lee, and Shillock, Revs. U. V. Pigot, G. B. Thurston, B. Percival, and L. J. T. Harwell, Dr. White J.P., Messrs. Z. Armit- age J.P., R. Garnett, J.P. R. I. Wynne Jones, W. Cop- Pock, W. Walker, R. G. Efenshall,J. Fairhurst,J. Geddes J.P. J. Fulton Smith (treasurer), and J. Ryan ksecre- ar The MAYOR in formally opening the show said that he con . ratulated the hon. sec., Mr. Ryan,and the commit- tee for the successful way in which they had carried out the arrangements. He only hoped and trusted that they I might see their way to continue the show annually. Be was perfectly certain that they gave great satisfaction and pleasure to the inhabit ants of Warrington. He was sure that it was a great incentive to exhibitors to try and compete with each other to bring their fruit and vegetables to as high a state of perfection as possible.. When a man was at work at his garden early in the morning, they might be sure that he would enjoy his breakfast. (Laughter and applause.) Alderman BOLTON on behalf of the committee thanked the Mayor for his presence that day. He said that there was a great improvement upon last year, as they had over 100 entries, and he thought that the quality of the plants and vegetables was better. He was Bare that the inhabitants of Warrington would appreciate the efforts of the committee,and next year they hoped to do better than this year. After the formal opening, the company proceeded to examine the various exhibits. During the afternoon the proceedings were enlivened by music, the musicians be- ing the Misses Birtles, Mr. Spinks, Miss and Master Randall, Miss Faull, and Mr. Percy Randall.

CLASS 3. COTTAGES.

PLANTS—Three window pints, distinct (special prize, copper kettle, given by Councillor Smethurst) : 1, T. Starkie •2, F. Childs. WARRINGTON AMBULANCE BRIGADE. PRESENTATION TO MIL H. PRITCHARD.

-- The first annual meeting of the above Association was held in Wycliffe Schools on Thursday last week and was largely attended. Mr. J. C. Parr,d.P., C.C., D.L., occupied the chair and was accompanied on the platform by Mr. R. Parr, Dr. and Mrs. Adams, Mr. T. Morris, F.G.S., Mr. C. H. Broadbent, Captain F. M. Appleton, the Rev. H. V. Piga, Mr. H. Pritchard (late hon. secretary), and Mr. T. H. Hardy (the present secretary). Dr. ADAMS read letters of apology for non-atten- dance from Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P. (president of the brigade), Mr. B. Fairclough, Captain Waring, Dr. Darwen, and Mr. A H. Crosfield who enclosed a postal order for 14 guineas, a gift which was very enthusiastically received. The CHAIRMAN said he was sure that they would join with him in regretting that their president was unable to be there. They all knew his heart was with them and they knew he would have been there if he could have been in two places at once. Unfortunately it was as yet impossible and Mr. Pierpoint had his Parliamen- tary duties to attend o. There were perhaps no sub- jects of more practical importance or more deserving of their encouragement and attention than the one they were met that evening to promote and organise ; for it involved the possible saving of human life and the relief or mitigation of human suffering,and when they looked around them and saw the multifarious objects which civilisation and science surrounded them with, which might at any moment cause accidents more or less seri- ous—our railways, tramways, manufactories, bicycles, and a hundred and one other things which might an sr moment cause complications—it was surely incumbent upon them to study the means of meeting and counter- acting the dangers which surrounded them on every side. (Applause.) To keep out of danger was altogether impossible and to avoid accidents was nearly as impossible. But to know what to do and how to do it when accidents occurred was a subject well worthy of their careful study, and a science—for he would call it a science—which it behoved them all to know a little of. Many lives had been saved and much suffering mitigated, or soothed, or avoided,by timely as- sistance and presence of mind, and on the other hand many valuable lives,so to apeak,had been thrown away, and much suffering had resulted from incapacity and ignorance in acting,or abstaining from acting on the spur of the moment. In cases of emergency the first natural feeling of alarm and nervous excitement at the occur- rence of an accident was increased and intensified by the feeling of helplessness to render any effective assistance, while on the contrary a small amount of practical or technical experience steadied the hand, and gave a con- fidence which no amount of well-intentioned zeal and kind anxiety to be useful could furnish. (Hear, hear.) The old proverb of' A stitch in time,etc.," was perhaps never more forcibly illustrated than in connection with the subjects before them, and the "first help" which it was the object of their Society to teach them how to render most efficiently and effectively might be the " stitch in time to rescue a fellow creaturefrom an un• timely grave, and to turn the scale of life and death with a precious human life pending in the balance. (Hear, hear.) Re was sure the association would be looked at in no light manner and he hoped many might be able to assist when called upon in emergency. All ought to know not only what to do but how to do it at the proper time. (Hear, hear.) SECRETARV'S REPORT. Mr. HARDY, the secretary then read his report which read as follows : s. In presenting their report to you for this, the first year of the existence of the brigade, your council defire to express their great satisfaction at the progress which has been made since its formation. A preliminary meet- ing was held on the 19th April last year, at which there were present Messrs.flardy.Pritchard,Williams,Bunkley, Willcock, and one or two others, who I am sorry to say have not been amongst us since,but the remainder stuck to their guns and worked hard to bring the movement to the notice of all ambulance men within the district, with the result that by the 11th of July about a dozen more bad joined the small band of workers, and at our next meeting on the 19th of September we had 33 present. Since then we have advanced by leaps and bounds, till we have at the present time no less than 69 names upon the register. One new class has been held which was carried through very successfully, 17 candi- dates obtaining the first aid certificate, 9 passing for the second, and one for the third course, for which the Medallion of the Association is awarded,in fact all passed who presented themselves for examination, and we con- sider that that result reflects great crelit upon their in- structot s. Another class was then started early this year, but, owing to the fact that we had to clear out from our quarters and were unable to secure another suitable room to practice in, the examination of this class has been postponed until the next season larens. Your Committee desire t 9 express their deep sense of gratitude to those gentlemen who have so generously supported the movement financially, viz., Mr. J.liCharl- ton Parr, Rev. B. B. Prescott, Mr. Thomas Morris, and Mr. Andrew Rathbane, and to Mr. Robert Heaton for his generoustipahebac tabours aui rtl sywotoroguitrlfia obefee Honorary nsat rneet eit nco stretcher. in its t Yh Surgeonsidoguuh duty yor weretappreciationhei Committee servicesto alsof ao f rt teil ee hues clered cheerfully and gratuitously—like all work con- ducted for and by the Order—in devoting a pertiun of their valuable time to instructing others how to help and render first aid to those who are suffering from accident or sudden illness. We are thankful to be able to say that efficient first aid has been rendered in a large num- ber of cases by members of the Brigade. The most im- portant is that in which Mr. George Willcock. assisted by Mr. William Floughton, arrested the hemorrhage in the case of a man at Messrs. Houghtons% Sankey Wire Mills, who had ruptured a varicose vein in the leg, thereby undoubtedly saving the patient's life. There have also been a number of severe cases of frac- ture, ore in which a workman fractured his wrist on the Central Station, first aid rendered by Mr. Hardy assisted by Mr. J. G. Buckley: Mr. Buckley also had a case of fracture of the leg of a boy in Ohroy's Circus, another of a boy who gdt his leg fast between the wheel and body of a lorry in Winwick-road, and another of a boy suffering from comminuted fracture of the leg through a wall falling upon him; Mr. Hardy has rendered first aid in two severe cases of dog bites, and our Hon. Treasurer, Mr. Mills, rendered first aid to a boy in DallamlForge who was suffering from severe fracture of the skull and other injuries, and who is at present recov- ering. There are a number of other cases that cannot be mentioned, because members have neglected to send in their reports to the Secretary, but we are now in posses- sion of a proper case report book, and we must urge upon all members to send in their reports as soon as possible after they occur. Your Committee desire to express their hope that the members of the Police force, the Railway Co.'s employees, and the employees of other firms within the district will join us. In conclusion your Council desire to give great prominence to the fact that this great and humane work cannot be carried on and prosecuted to that extent, and with that vigour that its importance demands, without solid and substantial pecuniary aid from those to whom its large and invalu- able aims commend it, and to them they earnestly and confidently appeal for continued and increased support ; and they also hope that the Technical Education Depart- ment of the County Council will be able to see their way to make us a substantial grant, thus helping the mem- bers to help themselves le this most noble and unselfish cause."

STATEMENT OF ACCOUNTS.

Mr. W. BAMBER, one of the auditors, road the state- ment of accounts which showed that the total receipts during the year from all sources amounted to £l9 19s. 5d., and the expenditure £l5 7s. 7d., leaving a bal3nce in hand of £4 113. Bd. All the accounts as audited were in a satisfactory condition. Dr. ADAMS proposed that the two reports be carried. _

Mr. T. Monnts seconded ,and said that the Association was one of the most God-like institutions he knew. (Applause.) Parsons preaching in a church were not in it. (Loud laughter.) It was they who could help when it was needed and knew how to help. (Applause.) Referring to the testimonial presented to Dr. Adams a short time ago, Mr. Morris said he hoped that before the session was finished they would be able to give some other doctor a like recognition. The motion was then put to the meeting and carried unanimously.

THE PRESENTATION.

The CHAIRMAN in making a presentation of a St. John Ambulance Society's gold medallion, said he thought no wards of his were needed to recommend Mr. Pritchard to them. They had more experience than he had of the valuable services Mr. Pritchard had rendered to the Society, and it was a pleasure to him to be the medium for transmitting their gratitude to him. He then, amidst loud applause, presented the medallion to Mr. Pritchard. The presentation included the following, testimonial ; " Presented to Mr. Henry Pritchard as a token of respect in which he is held by every member, on the occasion of his removal from amongst us to another sphere of labour and as a slight reward for his services rendered in support of the movement since its commencement, when he was one of its promoters." Mr. Prugcminn, in responding, expressed his deep gratitude to the members for the present, and said that what little he had done was not worthy so valuable a present. What he had done he had done for a sense of duty—working on the railway as he did—where an ac- cident might occur at any moment. His six years in Warrington had been a time of pleasure andtbe memory of the kindness he had received would never die as long as he lived.

===== 0002601_18950831_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF OFFICERS.

All the officers of the past session were re-elected un- animously. There were no changes or additions. After a most interesting and instructive exhibition of ambulance brigade drill by a number of the members,all of whom showed praiseworthy efficiency, Dr. ADAMS said that the Warrington brigade had, more by lapse than intention, almost ceased to be a centre. It was suggested to re-constitute the Warring- ton centre of the Association, and for that they would require a full staff of officials. He himself had the honour of succeeding Mr. C. H. Broadbent as hoe. sec. of the Warrington centre, and it would be his duty and pleasuye to promote the interests of the Association. While the centre was in abeyance ambulance work had grown up round it and they had felt themselves strong enough to organize a brigade, as it were "on their own hook." He had been informed that the Warrington Ambulance Brigade to conform with the regulations would have to read as the Warrington Corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade. The centre, he hoped would include sub sections of rural townships such as Stretton and Penketh. He therefore proposed that the Warring- ton centre of the St. John Ambulance Association be re- constructed, and that the question of its constitutional formation in regard to officers and committee be referred to a special committee to be formed later on. Mr. C. H. BROADBENT seconded the resolution, and referred in felicitous terms to his successor, Dr. Adams. He also heartily congratulated the secretary,Mr. Hardy, on the membership and the keenness the members dis- played, and the progress the brigade had made. What they must all try to do was to induce others to take an interest in the work—(hear, hear,)—so that they might have as many certificate holders, and eventually as many medallion holders as possible. (Applause.) He was glad to see such a good attendance and so many ladies, and took it as good augury for the future.

The resolution was ihen carried. A PRIZE WON.

The CRAIRMAN explained that a handsome silver cup on his table was presented by the Sunlight Soap manu- facturers, and formed the 3rd prize in an open competi- tion for the Cheshire Lines employes held at Liverpool, and won by the detachment from the Warrington Central Station. (Applause.) As a matter of further pride all the competitors were members of the Brigade. (Loud applause'.) Mr. MORRIS moved that a hearty vote of thanks be acccrded the chairman of the meeting. The RPv. H. V. PIGOT seconded.

Mr. PARR, who was applauded vigorously,thanked all very much for their kind recognition of his small ser- vices that evening. He was pleased to act for Mr. Pier. point, who he hoped would be able to preside on the next occasion. It was a great encouragement to see such a hearty meeting composed of young and old, and even babies—(laughter),—who he was sure would in course of time become useful members of the Associa- tion. (Loud applause.) The proceedings then terminated.

WARRINGTON LEAGUE OF THE CROSS. VIGOROUS CRUSADE AGAINST INTEMPERANCE. The visitors, and councillors, together with the execu- tive, of the Warrington League of the Cross, made a house to house visitation throughout the various districts of the town on Sunday. The visits were not only made to those already initiated into the League, but to moat of the Catholic houses in the town, Miniature temperance meetings were held in these houses,and the evils of intemperance were pointed out,in plain, simple, but forcible language. The people were earnestly exhorted to throw in their lot with the temper- ance cause, and to work no longer to enrich a gigantic monopoly, which spelt for them ruin, misery, and de- gradation, but to work for their homes, their families, and themselves. Needless to add the visitors and their co-workers were received courteously and the people seemed grateful for the interest evinced in their welfare. As a natural consequence of this work, the League Hall was crowded on Sunday night, many of those who had promised to turn over a new leaf. putting in an appear- ance, and were a source of gratification to the Council and ordinary members of the League who attended in large numbers, many finding it impossible to obtain seats and were obliged to stand during the whole of the time of the meeting.

Mr. Joint MULLINS, president, who presided with his usual ability, gave an instructive address on the neces- sity of societies like the League of the Cross,for working men. He pointed out the dull surroundings of many of the slum homes in the town, which the poorer people are on account of their poverty obliged to occupy and referred to the great attraction there was for men tired and wearied after a week of monotonous toil,in the well lighted, bright, and cheerful public-houses in the town. The League of the Cross was in a very humble way intended AS a counter attraction to these places of iniquity. Workingmen could find bright, cheerful rooms in the League, where papers were free, books free, and good companionship could always be had. He strongly urged all Catholics to sign the pledge and safeguard themselves against the terrible consequences attending the frequentation of the "pleasant dens" devoted to the certain ruin of too many of our poor people. Mr.T. C. DALTON addreesed the meeting on the subject of the visitation. He said some of those who had been invited to join the League, and by this act to turn their backs once and for aye on the universal enemy of the workingman,had pleaded their inability to shake off this evil and destroying habit. In other words,they confessed themselves slaves to this habit, this fearful vice of in- temperance. Let them be sure of one thing, and it was this, no man who tried earnestly to overcome this evil habit ever failed. The means at hand in such a society as the League of the Croo.,vvere an infallible and certain cure of the most depraved case existing in their midst. The means were, 1 : Daily prayer to the great and good God, who had said, "Ask and you shall 'receive " ; 2 Monthly Communion in a body 3 . to work for the interests of the League of the Cross, by bringing in new members. He then went on to point out the fearful evils accruing from the drinking habits of the people. Many, very many, of the Cath,lic houses in Warrington had not a representative at the Holy Mass on the Lord's Day, and yet they were bound to go to Holy Mass in order to sanctify the Lord's Day. Be contrasted the indifference the Catholic people le this tewn,with the life of the monk in his cloister, by giving a detailed account of the day's work of a member of the Carthusian order. They (th. monks) rose each day at 5.30 a.m., and were engaged in devotions till 12 o'clock noon. prom September the 14th to Easter they eat but tWo meals a day, and from Easter to September the 14th they eat but one ! After a hard day's work during which strict silence was observed, they retired to rest at 7 p.m., and anyone passing a Carthusian monastery, would at a little before eleven o'clock every night hear the sweet tone of the Matin bell. At eleven o'clock the monks commenced to offer up prayers of praise and thanksgiving, and supplicated the great God to have mercy on a sinful and wicked world. These devotions lasted until two o'clock in the morning, and then the Holy monks retired to rest, commencing their day again at 530 a.m. During their lives they never eat any flesh meat,their only means of subsistence being bread, fish, cheese, fruit and vegetables.

The Rev..RATII. RYIN, in administering the life pledge to seven men. said there was no greater slavery than the slavery of drink. If a man was an actual slave ruled by a master who owned him as his property, the slave would still have his senses, would be tolerably well fed, clothed, and housed, but the slave of drink robbed himself of his senses, was hungry and ragged and in every way despicable. But, worst of all the drunk- ard WAR a slave of the Devil. The rev, gentleman ex- horted the men to be faithful to their pledge, and as a means to that end to prepare for their religious duties by fervent prayer and whole-hearted service to their God and His holy church.

A Blackburn cyclist named Wake, whilst crossing the electric tramway. near Portrush, was thrown front his machine, and fell on the rail through which the current is transmitted. Death was instantaneous ; and the juts' at the inquest on Tuesday found that it was due to elec- tric shock.

MAN GORED TO DEATH BY A BULL.

On Tuesday afternoon the Flintshire Comer (Mr. R. Bromley) held an inquest at Faenol Fawr Farm, Bodel- wyddan, touching the death of an old man named Edward Jones, residing at Ty Cuaf, Towyn, Abergele, who was killed on Monday under sad circumstances.— David Jones, 1, Gwloch-at., Llandudno, identified the deceased as his fatker,who was 74 years of age.—Thomas Williams, of Towyn,said on Monday morning about ten o'clock he was working in a ditch in company with the deceased. They would be about two or three fields away from Faenol Fawr. The deceased picked up a pikel and got out of the ditch to drive away a. bull which was standing at the edge knocking into the ditch the dirt which witness and deceased were excavating. The animal was roaring and appeared to be enraged. De- ceased commenced to poke at the bull with the pikel,and it backed for about twenty yards. The next thing he saw—everything happened so quickly—was the bull taking deceased on its horns and throwing him some distance away. Witness went towards the farm for as- sistance.—Peter Williams,son of the last witness,said he saw the deceased pricking the bull, It then rushed at him and knocked him down.—William Vaughan, Ty Twynch, Rhyddlau, said he saw the bull threw deceased into the air. It then rushed at him and tossed him into the ditch.—Police Constable John James deposed to dis- covering the deceased lying in the ditch on his face. He was in an almost nude condition,the clothes having been ripped off him. He was terribly bruised, and his ribs were smashed in. There was also a large gash across the lower part of the abdomen. The body was very much swollen.—Mr. William Owen, tenant of Faenel Fawr Farm, said the bull belonged to him. It nas not- more vicious than bulls usually were. The deceased no doubt enraged it with the pikel.—The Coroner, in sum- ming up, said fortunately such cases were very rare. From the evidence it appeared that deceased very foolishly, especially at his age, tried to drive away an enraged animal with a weapon that only seemed to in- furiate it the more,with the result that he lost his life.— The jury returned a verdict of " Accidentally killed by being gored with a bull."—Mr. Owen said he would take care that the bull was kept within bounds in tke future. He would like to state, however, as it had not come out in evidence, that the pikel points were covered with blood, which showed conclusively that the deceased had attacked the bull before it had attacked hiln.

WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL. MEETING OF THE CHAMBER OF COMMERCE AND LOCAL TRADERS.

We understand that at the monthly meeting of the Chamber of Commerce held on Monday afternoon at Market-gate Chambers, the proposed arrangement be- tween Warrington Corporation and the Ship Canal Com- pany was fully discussed, and that a resolution was passed approving generally of the proposals submitted by the Rivera' Committee as the basis of settlement pro- vided that adequate security was taken for their fulfil- ment. The Council, however, suggested that having re- gard to the extent of the concessions which the town was making in reference to dredging, and in relieving the Canal Company from their obligation to construct a dock at Warrington they should be asked to remove the 240 tons limit on river, as well as on coast-wise traffic, and in case the Corporation are to undertake the risk of constructing a dock themselves,to allow them to partici- pate, upon some equitable basis, in any profit which might ultimately accrue therefrom. Various minor sug- gestions were also made, and it is understood that these will be communicated to the Rivers Committee at their next meeting. A committee of the Warrington Traders' Association met on Tuesday afternoon at the offices of Messrs. Green- all and Buckton, Horaemarket-street, and took into con- sideration the proposals of the Ship Canal Company. The proceedings were conducted in cantera,and the ulti- mate result of the conference was that the committee will arrange to confer with the Corporation as soon a?, possible.

HOITSEBREAKING BY BOYS.

EXEMPLARY PENALTY.

On Saturday morning at the Warrington Boron& Police Court before Mr. John Burgess (chairman), Mesas. John Fairclough and F. Monks, John Peter Hines, lop, Pierpoint-street, and John Bolton, 62, Pierpoint-st., both boys of 15, were charged with stealing from 96, Bewsey- road on Monday two ladies' bodices, the peoperty of Samuel J. Beaman, and also a gas bracket and pipe, and bunch of keys, the property of Mary Senior. Mr. F. R. Roberts prosecuted on behalf of Mrs. Senior, and stated that on Monday last his client, who lived next door to the house in question, formerly occupied by Councillor Beaman, heard a noise, and going rend to the back of the house saw that the window panes had been cut out of the scullery window. She got her keys and went in and saw the two prisoners at work in the kitchen without shoes or stockings on.a bundle contain- ing the various articles of apparel was on the floor ready to be taken away and the gas brackets were also placed ready to be conveyed away. Immediately prisoners saw her coming they attempted to eet out of the window—to pass through which they must have ex- ercised considerable ingenuity—but filled. Thev then turned round and rushed past her out of the front door and escaped.lnformation was given to Detective-Sergeant Monaghan who visited the house and found that prisoners had left their clogs and a cap behind, and it was by this means that Monaghan was afterwards able to identify them. Detective-Sergeant Mcnaghan said that he examined the premises on Monday afternoon and found the win- dow as described. On Tuesday night about 11.30 he visited the home of each of the prisoners but found that they were gone. On Friday night he again visited the houses and found them at home and brought both to the police station. Hines said that the clogs were his and Bolton confessed to owning the cap. When charged, Hines said they got through the window and went up- stairs and found the clothes and brought them down. Bolton said they did not take the gas bracket but found it in a cupboard. Mr. Roberts here mentioned that his client was cnIT anxious to protect her property and did not wish to press the case. If their worships would deal with the prisoners in a manner calculated to deter them from that kind of thing in the future he would be perfectly ccntent.

In Court prisoners said they went into the house after rags. The Chief Constable,in passing to the Bench the char- acter sheet et prisoners, said they were the terror of the neighbourhood,and seemei to have been banded together for some time. Cottage after cottage had been broken into and things taken but of no great value. Both had also stood before the Bench before. It was also explained that the clothes had been left in the house by Mr. Beaman wheri he left. The magistrates sent the prisoners to gaol for one month's hard labour each.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

D RUNKENNEER. At Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday morning be- fore Mr. F. J. Norman, a well known Runcorn character named Harriet Baxter, was charged with being drunk in Church-st. at eleven o'cleck the previous n•ght. P C. Kendrick proved the case, and in default of pa% ing the fine of 10s. and costs, which was imposed,she was com- mitted to prison for 14 days. ACCIDENT TO A SHIP CARPENTER AT RUNCORN. A serious accident betel the carpenter engaged cin the Bolivia, at present moored at the Runcorn lay-by. It appears that on Saturday he was on the middle deck when a log from the upper deck tell upon him, knocking him into the hold. Dr. McKenzie was called in, and that gentleman found him suffering from a severe scalp wound and a broken arm. FINE APPLE GROWING NEAR RUNCORN. Three excellent specimens of the Lent Suffield apple are at present in the possession of Mr. H. Darlington, of the Railway Hotel,Runcorn. They have been grown by his son near Overton on young trees and they weigh on an average, one pound each. A photograph has been taken of them for reproduction in an illustrated garden- ing journal.

PICNIC FROM RUNCORN. The teachers of the Nelson-st.,Wesleyan Mission School and the choir at Camden Wesleyan Church had their annual picnic on Saturday. They were conveyed in Messrs. Yarwood's waggonettes to Sutton Hall, where they were joined by the Sutton Weaver choir. A num- ber of games were engaged in. The proceedings were varied hay the singing of glees and anthems, under the conductorship of Mr. S. Bazley. Mr. John TemperieV presided at the piano. During their stay, to a was par- taken of, the tables being presided over by Mrs. Lowe. Mrs. Bazley, Mrs. Lovett, and Miss Harrison. On the preposition of Mr. J. Bazley, seconded by Mr. A. Harri- son, supported by Mr. Lambert, and Mr. Nichnlas. a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. and Mrs Lowe, who had entertained them for a number of years. A most enjoyable time was spent. A WELL-KNOWN CHARACTER FROM WARRINGTON. Martin Lyons, a hawker of Warrington, whose ap- pearances at Warrington. Runcorn, and other police courts have been numerous, was again an occupant of the prisoners' dock at Runcorn Police Court,on Tuesday, this time charged with being drunk and disorderly in Bridge-street. Runcorn, the previous day. From the evidence adduced it appears that the attention or the police was called to the prisoner by a magistrate, and owing to Lyons' disorderly conduct he had to be lea kad up.—P.C. Ellis and Sergeant Gill stated the case and in- formed the Bench that the prisoner was very drunk.— Inspector Hicks said they bad 34 recorded appearances before the magistrates against the prisoner. A fine of 403. and costs or one month's imprisonment with hard labour was inflicted. The magistrates were Mr. P. Speakman and Mr. E. unison. THE SAD SCALDING FATALITY AT RUNCORN. Mr. J. E. Worsley held an inquest at Runcorn Police Station, on Friday afternoon, relative to the death of Calvert Grisdale, 11 months old, son of William Gris- dale, 12, York-at.' Runcorn. Mrs. Grisdale was the first witness called and stated that on the evening of the 20th inst, she bad the child under her right arm, and on her lifting a kettle from the fire, the baby laid hold of the handle of the saucepan and upset it. The pan con- tained boiling water and onions, and the child was scalded c n the back with the contents. She ran into a neighbour's house with it, and some vaseline was put upon its back. It was not very badly scalded, but the child died on Thursday at noon. It was attended by Dr. McLennan's assistant. —Mrs. Read gave evidence as to seeing,the child after it had been scalded, and to putting vaseline on its back.—Dr. Gilchrist said hems called in to see the child on Tuesc ay night about nine o'clock. He attended the child until its death on Thursday. Shock was the cause of death, consequent on the scalding. A verdict of "Accidental death ' was returned. TEB NAVY MISSION—PRESENTATION AT RUNCORN. About fifty workers and friends assembled at the Mariner's Church, Irwell-lane, Runcorn, on Monday evening to bid farewell to Mr. G. Sutton ho is leaving Runcorn for Maesteg, S. W to labour in'the Navy Mis- sion Society. Owing to Mr. Sutton's successful work in Runcorn he has been placed. on the society's staff of missionaries without undergoing any term of probation. The meeting was presided over by Mr. W. Shaw,mission- ary, and during the proceedings he called upon Mrs. Foster to make a presentation to Mr. Sutton. It con- sisted of a writing desk, and in presenting it, she said it had been the unanimous wish of all present that they should show in a tangible form their esteem of Mr. Sutton.—Mr. Sutton, in acknowledging the gift, said no one could have had a more zealous and willing band of workers. He hoped they would still carry on the work of the Mariner's Church.—The Chairman afterwards made some appropriate resnarks,and referred to Ws early career in the Mersey Mission work.—Mr. D. Smith spoke of the changes which had taken place in Runcorn since the commencement of the Navvy Mission in that place seven years ago. nab was the third occasion on vhich they had met to shake hands with members who were going to be missionaries. There was no doubt that the society's work at Runcorn had been a groat blessing and of a lasting character. It was gratifying to know that both Mr. John Langley and Mr. John Dew who had been connected with them at Runcorn were successful in their mission work.—A pleasant evening terminated about ten p.m. THE LATH MR. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 31, 1895

A RUNCORN MISSIONARY ON FURLOUGH.

The pulpit at Holy Trinity Church, Runcorn, was on Sunday occupied morning and evening by the Rev. F.C. Salkeld, vicar of Arsonal, Bengal. The rev, gentleman, who is the s.cond on of Mr.W. F.Salkeld,of Heath-road, Runccrn, has come over for the purpose of recruiting his health,which was being seriously affected by his labours in India. He is well known by Runcornians,and for some time was assistant to Dr. Robinson, Many of his old acqqaintances went to hear him preach on Sunday, and his discourses were greatly appreciated. PICNIC TO CHESTER.

The members of the Bethesda Christian Endeavour Society had a picnic to Chester on Wednesday. About thirty left Runcorn shortly after ten o'clock in a wagon- ette supplied by Mr. Yarwood. Alter a most pleasant drive they arrived at the ancient city About noon. Dinner was partaken of, after which the party took a steamer for E.;closton Ferry. Here they spent a few pleasant hours in rambling through Eaton Hall Park, and after- wards had tea. Runcorn was again reached soon after ten o'clock, all having thoroughly enjlyed the outing. The play was accompanied by the pastor (the Rev. J. Simpson. DEATH OF MRS. BAZLEY.

Many will regret to bear of the death of Mrs. Bazley, wife of. Joseph Bazley, of Vine-street, Runcorn, which areured somewhat suddenly on Wednesday evening. She had attained the age of 56 years, and was a native of Runcorn, being a daughter of the late Mr. James Yates. She was formerly attached to Bethesda Congregational School, hut after marriage she joined the Wesleyan hodist Society. Forsome years she was a class leader at Camden Wesleyan school, and on relinguishing this she engaged in various duties connected with the insti- tution. She was well-known in the town, especially in Wesleyan circles. During the early part of the week she became ill and died as stated from affections of the heart.

RUNCORN CYCLE CLUB RACE.

In connection with the Runcorn Cycle Club, a ten mile race over High Legh course was held on Saturday aft-rnoon. Nine members participated, and the three prizes to be competed for consisted of first,a gold medal ; second, silver medal with gold centre ; and 3rd, cycle stand. A btrong head wind was blowing at the time and this was not at all favourable to good racing. The first prize was captured by W. Stubbs, his time being 41s minutes. H. Smith was second and T. Grounds third, and S. Miles made a good fourth, the three cyclists com- ing in aim lst abreast. The judges were Messrs. T. Kirkham and J. Draper, and the starter was Mr. W. Wis wall. VOLUNTEERS AT CHURCH. The members of the Runcorn companies of the Earl of Cheater's Regiment attended divine service at the Parish Church, Runcorn, on Sunday morning. They marched from their headquarters headed by their brass band,and amingst those present were Colonel Ashton, Captains Harrison, and G. F. Ashton ; Lieutenant Brundrit, Quartermaster-Sergeant Hankinson, CoLour-Sergeant Poole. There were also seven sergeants, 58 rank and file, and 24 members of the band. The Rev. Canon Maitland Wood preached an appropriate sermon. On returning to the Drill Hall, Colonel Ashton expressed his regret that the members had not mustered in stronger force. He congratulated those who had attended upon their smart appearance, and referred to their success at camp.
