Mr. J. E. Wora4 helciaiiWo;si at the Oak Inn, Weston, on Thursday morning into the circum- stances attending the death of a child, six days old, daughter of Jane Weodhead, of 7, Riley's Row, Weston. —The sister of the mother stated that it was apparently all right on Tuesday morning, and she took it upstairs ,)1, about ten o'clock to its mother will ,II asleep, and on awakrning found that the child's 'f ,presented a singu- lar appearance.—lsabella Best wh 'wall present at the birth of the child was called .oiit and pronounced the child to be dead. It was a healthy child.—Dr. McDougall deposed that he had made an external examination of the body and in his opinion death was due to asphyxia or overlaying. It was due tothe reprehensible practice of mothers allowingltheir children to be with them. After the clothes got over it, a few minutes were sufficient to cause death. There were no marks of violence or ankthing beyond overlaying, which caused the death of the child.—The Coroner commented upon the habit of mothers having their children in bed with them. He said they ought to have a crib or lay the little one upon the pillow or outside the bed clothing. So long as mothers would have them covered up in bed with them, there would be such cases to inquire into A verdict that the child was accidentally overlain in bed whilst with its mother was recorded. • THE RECENT CYCLE PARADE.

A meeting in connection with the Runcorn Cycle Club was held on Thursday evening at the headquarters of the club for the purpose of considering the balance sheet of the recent parade. Mr. W. Shaw (missionary) pre- sided and there was a good attendance of cyclists. Mr. H. Wright presented the financial statement from which it appeared that the amount collected was £4O 123. 7d. which together with the sale of programmes and adver- tisements brought the total receipts to £46 7s. 71. After moeting all expenses there was a balance in hand of £4l 10. 101. The balance sheet was adopted, and it Wai decided that the balance in hand should be divided eqoally between the National Lifeboat Institution, and the Runcorn District Nurse Fund. Votes of thanks hav- ing been passed to the treasurer and secretaries the meeting terminated.

THE WIDNES ELOPEMENT.

At Liverpool Counts' Quarter Sessions, on Tuesday, William Rutherford (33): barber, WAS indicted for hav- ing at Widnes, on the 3011 July, stolen a quantity of household furniture, belonging to Nicholas Farrell.—Mr. Formby, who appeared for the prosecution, said that at the time or the alleged theft the prisoner was lodging with the Farells in Wareing-street, Widnes. It appeared that Farrell left his home about 1.30 on the after- noon of the 30 h July, leaving his wife and the prisoner in the house. When he returned home from work at ten o'clock at night he discovered that almost the whole of the furniture had been taken away, and that his wife and the lod,g,r had disappeared. In so many worth, counsel added, the rrisoner was charged with having eloped with Mrs. Farrell. The husband informed the police of what had occurred, and afterwards, accom- panied by a constable, he went to a house in Titch- tield-street, Liverpool. The police officer went into the house, and there found Mrs, Farrell. The greater portion of the stolen furniture was also there. Prisoner was in an upstairs room, where subsequently a Teat honk was fcund, made out in the prisoner's name.—Dr. Commins for the &fence, contended that the furniture was the property of Mrs. Farrell, and denied that any- thing in t'ne nature of an elopement had occurred. Mrs. Farrill, not being comfortable with her husband, re- moved to Liverpool, and prisoner stayed there simp'y as a lodger.—Mrs. Farrell gave evidence on oath to this effect, and denied that anything improper had taken place between herself and the prisoner.—The jury found the prisoner guilty, and he was sent to prison for three months.

===== 0002601_18950831_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INSPECTOR'S REPORT.

The Clerk read Her Majesty's Inspectors' reports on the schools. For Mill Brow Schools there was no report on the general work inasmuch as there was no examina- tion. The grant was given on the same scale as the pre- vious year, namely 17s. per head which with reductions totalled £96 7s. 6d. The report for the evening school read : This is a distinctly excellent evening school. The work reflects the greatest credit on the headmaster (Mr. Moulton). The girls' school has made a great advance, and is doing good work which deserves every praise. All the subjects are well taught and the needlework lessons deserve special praise." Mr. Moss said the amount earned was £B4 17s. 61., but the grant really should be £lO5 13s. 61. The Department had taken £2O 16s. off as the grant was over 17s. 6d. per head.—Canon Wood said it was a most unreasonable rule. When they did well the Department said it was creditable and then cut the grant down.—Mr. Taylor remarked that it was not very 'encouraging.—Canon Wood : It certainly is not. The report of the drawing examination at the Greenway- road Board (boys') School was" good." The Clerk said that would be is. 6d. per head on the average attend- ance.—Canon Wood: What was it last year P—The Clerk: " Excellent."—Canon Wood : Is the headmaster satisfied with the report ?—The Clerk : He is most dis- tinctly dissatisfied. In his opinion it is not a just report. Canon Wood : Wasn't it last year that the papers were re-examined P—The' Clerk : It was two years ago. With regard to the Greenway-road Board School, there was no examination of the boys, and therefore no re- port on the work of the year. The grant was to be at the same rate as the previous year, viz., 20s. 6d. In addition to the ordinary grant,for the first time a special subject had been taught—algebra. 63 boys were examined and passed" excellent," the amount of grant being £9 9s. The girls were examined, and the report stated that the school was as usual in excellent order, and the general character ot the work was good. The school accommodation was at present insufficient for the average attendance. That should be at once remedied, or the grants next year would be endangered.—The Clerk stated that the attendance was 30 or 40 over the average during the previous year.—Mr. Taylor thought it would have been more businesslike if the reports had been considered by the School Management Committee before submitting them to the Board.—Dr. McDougall said : Who was responsible for the overcrowding but the board school staff? They had no right to admit child- ren and overcrowd the schools when they knew the ac- commodation there was. There was plenty of room else where.—Canon Wood thought they had sufficient evi- dence that it was not convenient to discuss the reports in public until they had studied them.—Mr. Taylor sup- ported this view, and the reports were then referred to the School Management Committee for consideration.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BT OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

At the risk of its being stated that I am ventilat- ing an old question, and one that has often been thought of,l venture to refer to the necessity of an accident hospital at Runcorn. This week a circum- stance occurred which unmistakeably showed the necessity for such an institution. A marine fireman had the misfortune to sustain injury at Saltport,and his mates brought him in an unconsoious condition to Runcorn, presumably in the hope that he would receive medical treatment. If these were their thoughts, they were soon to be made conscious of their mistake, for after an examination by Dr. Mc- Kenzie it was &mind necessary that the injured man should be sent to a hospital, and he was accord- ingly despatched in the charge of Mr.Shaw,mission- ary, to the Royal Infirmary, Liverpool, where the unfortunate fellow expired soon after admission. This is not one solitary instance, for serious acci- dents are occurring quite frequently in and around Runcorn,and when the sufferers cannot possibly be treated in a dwelling-house, the inevitable railway journey to Liverpool has to be undertaken, and what that means only the sufferer knows.

In a progressive town like Runcorn this is a regrettable fact, and is against the spirit of the times. Runcorn people have shown themselves charitable enough to support a district nurse in their midst, and it is now for them to go one step further and establish u cottage hospitce—it need not be on a very large scale—in the town. There are numerous local gentlemen who would,no doubt, be willing to subqcribe towards such an object, and as to the cost of maintaining the institution, there might be adopted amongst the various Christian denominations, as is the case in many towns, a yearly Hospital Sunday.

I am glad to hear that the meeting in connection with the Runcorn Co-operative Society held on Tuesday for the purpose of again considering the rules, was characterised by more good feeling and more unanimity than has been the case with its predecessors. The Runcorn mem. hers acceded to some of the requisitions of the Widnes members and probably this will tend tO more harmony and co- operation in the future. For instance,there is now to be held- at Widnes the half-Yearly meeting of the society, and on the other hand, the annual meeting will be held at Runcorn. Another matter of importance is the increase decided upon in the number of members of the committee. The com- mittee in future will consist of twelve instead of eight, and they are to be elected by ballot in lieu of the " show of hands "which has been in vogue up to the present. This has been agitated for by the Widnes members for some time, and now that they can see that there is a disposition on the part of the Runcorn members to accede to any resonable suggestions, they will probably talk less of separa- tion.

The most important business brought before the meeting of the Runcorn School Board on Tuesday was the Inspectors' reports of the recent examina- tions. They were,on the whole, very satisfactory, and in this respect the Board schools have, since their establishment in Runcorn, occupied a high position. The Evening Continuation School, which may be described as a bridge between the Elemen- tary and Technical Schools, is stated in the report to he a "distinctly excellent school," a term which must be gratifying to all concerned. A feature worth noticing, which to use a well-known phrase, " takes the gilt off the gingerbread," is the " reduc- tions." The grant given by the Feducation Depart- ment is limited,and although more than the limited grant was actually earned, it is kept back. This fixing of the limit does not. always work out to the advantage of Board schools as compared with Voluntary schools, for it may be safely said that the Board schools the more frequently top the limit, and yet are not able to show any monetary advan- tage for doing so.

To-day (Saturday) the Technical Institute is to be formally opened by Sir John Brunner Bart, M.P. Every effort is being put forth to make the event a memorable one in the history of Runcorn, and there is every likelihood of this being accomplished. From a glance at the syllabus which has been pre- pared for the forthcoming session, if. the various classes contemplated are formed, the Institute will soon justify its existence. In selecting the subjects, full consideration has been given to the occupations of the townspeople,and with a good staff of teachers and a model institute, there should be no lack of pupils. If the institute is made full use of, it will play an important part in the future of the town. Lnik.

MARION° ACCIDENT.—On Monday afternoon an acci- dent occurred in the heart of the village which caused great alarm. It appears that a party of excursionists had driven to the village in a wagonette.the property ,af Mr. George Roswell, nab proprietor, of Rusholme, Man- chester, and were putting up at the Bridgewatiir Hotel. The driver of the vehicle, a man named Roger Booth, had tahen out the horSes, and was turning the horses round,when the ponderous vehicle overpowered him and ran down whitbarrow Bridge and into the palisading, which was smashed, the vehicle failing over the edge of the bridge into Bridge-st., and carrying Booth with it, who on h"ing picked up was found to be badly shaken, and suffering from internal injuries. F4irtunately, no persons were passing, beneath the bridge at the time of the oceurrence,or they would have been in great danger of being killed.

RUNCORN CO OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

THE ADJOURNED MEETING.

Under the presideney of Mr. Sayles, the adjourned meeting in connection with the above-named society was held in the Co-operative Hall, on Tuesday evening. There were also present :—Messrs. Riley, Rhodes,Aston, Dyer, Worthington, Dudley, and Evans (manager), and Slaaw (assiltant secretary). At the outset, Mr. Poulson asked a question which was ruled out of order by the chairman, as to whether the committee had lately been consistiug of nine members instead of eight, in conse- quence ni some objection having been raised to Mr. Ciley. The Chairman said they had been taking legal advice as to Mr. Riley's position, and denied that any other member had been taking his place on the com- mittee.

Rule 3, relating to the holding of meetings and voting was then taken into consideration. To this Mr. Fitz- patrick of Widnes moved an amendment that the half- yearly meeting in February be held at Widnes and the annual meeting in August at Runcorn.—Mr. W. H. Clarke seconded the amendment. Two suggestions made by Mr. Goodies were accepted by Mr. Fitzpatrick as additions to his amendment to the rule. They were that no meeting of the society should proceed to business unless at least fifty members were present instead of twenty as stated in the rule, and also that a special meeting should be called at the requisition of fifty members instead of ten as at present. He con- tended, with regard to the first suggestion, that twenty were not sufficient to proceed with the business of a society with such a large number of members as theirs. —On the amendment to the rule being put to the meet- ing it was carried by 67 votes to 33. Tne tellers for the counting of votes were Messrs. Wilkinson and James Johnson.

Rule 4 having reference to loans was also amended on the motion of Mr. Cozier,seconded by Mr. Jabez Abs. The rule was altered to read that the interest on loans should not exceed 5 per cent, instead of 6 per cent, as stated therein. Rules 5,6, and 7, relating to regiaira- don and transfer of shares, and withdrawal of members, were passed without comment. Rule 8 related to the auditing of the accounts. Instead of two or more members being appointed as auditors, I Mr. Dudley moved that a chartered accountant tie ap- pointed as auditor, stating that owing to the size of the 1 society and the amount of transactionstit was necessary to have a chartered accountant instead of members as auditors.—Mr. Cozier seconded the amendment, and it was carried.—An addition was made to the rule on the motion of Mr. Poulson, that the balance sheets should been distributed seven clear days before the meetings of members. His object in doing so was that the members should have an opportunity of studying the balance sheet before the meetings. . . . . .

Rule 9, dealing wit' the investment of capital, was next taken. Mr. Cozier proposed, and Mr. Stubbs se- conded that the following alteration be made:—" That the directors may loaner with the sanction of an ordin- ary or special meeting, may invest any part of the sur- plus capital in the security of any other society providod that it was a registered society,and other than a limited liability."—lt was moved by Mr. Bilcross, seconded by Mr. Goodier, that the rule stand as at present, which omitted the power to loan surplus capital. A vote was taken and the amendment to the rule was agreed to by 70 votes to 47. Rules 10 and 11, referring to the death of members, and the application of profits, were passed without alteration.

Rule 12 proved a very contentious one. It wasdecided that it should be discussed sentence by sentence. With regard to the first sentence, stipulating that the number of elected persons on the committee should be eight, an amendment was proposed by Mr. Poulson, seconded by Mr. W. H. Clarke, that 12 persons should compose the committee. Mr. Dudly in supporting the amendment stated that since sitting on the committee he found there was a difficulty in forming a quorum which was five. He thought a committee of eight was not sufficient to carry on the bu_iness of such a large society and he was in favour of the number being increased.—Mr. Worthington in propoiing that the rule remain as at present, said eight , had been found sufficient to manage the society for a number oi years and with success. He thought the present number was sufficient. Mr. Dyer seconded.— Another proposition was made by Mr. Fitzpatrick, and it was seconded by Mr. Elen.that ten members compose the committee. On being put to the vote there voted for eight members 20; for ten members, 56; and for twelve members. 82. The latter was therefore carried. —A long discussion then ensued as to the mode of elect- ing the members of the committee.—Mr. Poulson made a proposition that three members of the committee retire each half-year.—Mr. Cozier seconded.—Mr. Holt moved an amendment that six committee men should retire each year and be eligible for re-election at the annual meeting. This was defeated by 72 votes to 47. As to electing the cfommittee it was decided, on the motion of Mr. Poulson, seconded by Mr. Cozier, that the following regulations should be complied with. The nomination papers t,) he sent out to the following places of business Runcorn (central) Weston, and Frodsham, Widnes, (Ditton-road), and Appleton. The papers to be returned by post with thenominations to the secretary at Runcorn within seven days after being issued, and to be signed and endorsed by at least three members' and the list of candidates to be posted at the society's branches seven days before the elections. The election to be by ballot and to take place on the Saturday prior to the half- yearly or annual meeting at which the result of the poll should be declared. in amendment to the rule was made on the proposition of Mr. Dudley, that the treasurer and secretary remain in office during the pleasure of the members instead of being re-elected each year. At this point the meeting was adjourned for a week.

A TIMELY SERMON BY THE REV. DR. GASTALDI,

PERNICIOUS LITERATURE.

The Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, of Runcorn, had the honour of preaching at St. Anne's Catholic Church,Edgehill, Liver- pool, on Sunday evening, before Cardinal Vaughan. He took for his text: " How readest thou" (Luke x., 26). He said the western world was literally littered with literature ; all through the hours by day and night busy pens are writing and revolving presses are reproducing a ceaseless flow of thoughts and words. From the edition de luxe down to the modest leaflet,of making books there is no end. Reading is regarded as a necessity of life,with- out which life would be worthless. That being so, he thought it not inopportune that evening to put to them the question in the Gospel—how readest thou, what readest thou, and what are the results of that reading? Fin first proceeded to classify books in their order of in- fluence—religious, scientific, historical, and literary. Then the Catholic Church from the chair of divine authority says to the world' Of all these books which are good you may read, but of those bad ones you shall not read because if you do you shall morally die." Why? Because they are poisonous to the intellect, the will, the moral faculties, and all the more dangerous because they seem so often to be full of sweet reasonableness. They flatter human passions. As it is said in Genesis (viii 21) the imagination and thoughts of man's heart are prone to evil. From his youth passion is like a hidden spark that is enkindled by some breath to become a consuming fire and that breath of passion is often wafted from the printed page. But sec.suality is not the only evil which bad reading may develop. Through thus imbibing vicious principles the heart of man is alienated from Heaven,becauoe faith is shaken ; and faith cl,mands an bumble subjection of our intellect to Divine Revelation, although we do not comprehend it. Moralists are loud in their laments over the far-reaching and deadly effects of intemper- ance. Drunkenness is denounced from the chair of truth, from the platform, in scathing words of rhetorical declamation. Language runs riot in frenzied efforts to destroy this enemy of mankind. But the other enemy— bad literature—is more seductive, more far-reaching,' more lethal even than the terrible evil of excessive drink- ing. The preacher then adverted at length to various! , evidences afforded by the papers and by the daily life around us to show.that pernicious literature was responsi- ble for much of the present disorganisation of society,and notably responsible tor juvenile crime. In illustration Ihe instanced the recent case of two boys in England killing their mother that they might steal her money and go about enjoying themselves. The penny literature found in their room would go far to accomit for the awfully depraved state into which they had worked their young inindo. He therefore argued that not only was the Church, altogether right in having the Congregation of the Index to examine into, and if necessary condemn books, but it would be highly desirable—if not abso- lutely necessary—for the State to place a restriction on the literary output, at least so far as it comes in touch with the pliable mind of youth.

ATIILETICS.

CRICKET. A bad beginning often proves to be the forerunner of success and good luck. This seems to have been the case with the Warrington Cricket Club. They began the season by getting systematically defeated, but the wheel of fortune having given another turn, a continuous run of success has been the outcome of the vigorous play of the home team. Though the Bolton team, in their first match this season with Warrington notched such a res- pectable score, Warringtonians with a better team than on that occasion had good hopes of winning on Saturday; and those hopes were not misplaced, for a splendid vic- tory of 108 runs was secured.

The match of Saturday, unlike the season, began bril- liantly. Messrs. J. .1. Crosfield and Dr. Smith were first partnered, and with quick scoring from the former and slower hits trom the latter, the grand total of 96 was reached before Smrh was floored at 36. Mr. Crosfield stood his ground for a spell with Bates, but a change in the bowling enabled EL Taylor to receive a hot catch from one of his own balls. Bates compiled 24, but the remaining batsmen, exclusive of Hind and Griffin, not out, were rapidly dismissed at the hands of Halley, Mitchell, and Taylor. With seven wickets down War- rington had scored 151.

Bolton's inning's was inaugurated by Ellis being sent back with only a single run scored, the deliveries of Mr. J. J. Crossfield being too much for him. Taylor gave a hoist of 12 to the score, and C. Stanley, not out, one of 14, but the remaining players were unable to effect any- thing exciting—except lose their wickets, and that was one-sided excitement—against the smart bowling of Crosfield and Smith.

--- Mr. Crosfield was undoubtedly the hero of the match, 89 in addition to his excellent score of 60, his bowling figures were exceptional, as he took 7 wickets for 11 runs. All-round good fielding was displayed on the Warrington side,and their victory was a thoroughly meri- torious one.

The Warrington second team playing at Bolton in- flicted a defeat almost as crushing as that A the first I team, for with a similar loss of only 7 wickets they Com- piled 117 against the home teams' 35 for a like number ,of wickets. Mt,ssrs. C.R.Adamson,2B,J.W.Bibby,22,and M. Sileock, 19, were the biggest scorers for Warrington, while the only man who could reach double fic..turea on the home side was Balshaw, not out frr 10. ,till there is an excuse for such low scoring on t.,eir pArt,for Bibby and Duckworth fired in the shots with such regularity and precision that it would have taken more than ordin- ary ability to have knocked up a high score. More good fielding was shown and smart catches were made by Crompton, Unsworth, and J. Duckworth. The " lads fro' Bowton " will havo had quite enough of Warring- ton energy for some time to come.

Turning to junior clubs,we come to the Y.M.C.A.first team, who on Saturday played a match with Lostock Gralam,at Arpley. In saying that they played a match, I only use the werd in courtesy, for in point of fact,the game was not a match at all but an exhibition of scor- ing and bowling by the Christians. With one wicket to fall the borne team scored the respectable total of 64 runs, while Lostock Gralam, by dint of great exertion, scored 1, which added to 3 extras, made 4. Roddy and Woods were responsible for this,and if bowlers have any reason to be proud ,they have. Such a displly must have been highly edifying to the home spectators.

Meanwhile, the Y.M C.A. second team were batting vigorously on the ground of the Sankey Wesleyans their opponents for the afternoon. In this match, the second team, like the first, succeeded in winning the match with a substantial majority, closing their innings with 84 runs against the 58 of the Wesleyans. Bate,Weld- log, and Sanders hit out vigorously for the victors, while Morrall and Jones acquitted themselves creditably for the homeSterS. LOoSe bowling seems to have been very prevalent when the visitors secured 18 extras and the VVesleyans 13.

With their matches with Llstock Gralam and an key Wesleyan% the Y.M.C.A. first and second teams closed a very successful season. The first team have won

11, lost 2 and drawn 2, and 3 have been cancelled. The second team have won 10, lost 6, and drawn 1, and 2 have been cancelled. The averages of noth batting and bawling are very good, and quite up to the usual stan- dard, considering the state of the fields that have had to be played upon. White and Woods for the first team with batting and bowling respectively, and Sanders for the second team, especially coming out with goßd analysis, whilst Thompson follows close upon the ' heels of Woods for bowling. THE SUICIDE OF A. WARRINGTON

TRADESMAN.

REPORT OF TFIE INQUEST.

Mr.S.BrighOuse,county coroner,nn— Tuesday conducted an enquiry at the Parade Room of the Police Station, relative to the death of Edmund Johnson, Wilderspool- road, a master tinplate worker,who was found hanging, with a rope round his neck, from a beam in his work- shop. Mrs. Johnson, wife of deceased, sai that to the best of her knowledge he was 61 years of age. She last saw him alive on Sunday about 2 o'clock, when he left the house and went across to the workshop. Ralf-an-hour later .he went to the shop and saw him hanging by the neck from the beam. The only reason she could assign for his committing the act was that of money difficubiss. He had also been strange in his manner for same time and more so during the three days preceding his death. During the Bank Holiday he had hurt his back and head with falling upon some boxes when leaving the Isle of Man boat. During the last week a number of bills had come dem an ding money, and these he destroyed. Money that he should have paid on an account to Mr. Spencer Wallhead, solicitor, he expended in going to the Isle of Man. She did not think he would commit the act hehad dose.

George Billington, barman at the Norton Arms, saw the deceased on Sunday a little after two. Saturday was the first time he had seen deceased take beer, ;and he had a pint on Sunday. Witness had no suspicion that he would take his life. He thought it strange that de- ceased was in his shirt sleeves at that time of the day. Edwin Sykes, who had worked for the deceased, and last saw him alive at dinner time on Saturday said he knew deceas-d was in monetary difficulties. He had done no hard work in the shop for about three months. He paid all his employes on Saturday. Witness thought him strange in his manner and inattentive to his work. Henry Ashton,landlord of the Bridge Inn, said that he was called into the shop at 2.30 on Sunday to cut the body down. Deceased was suspended from a beam hav- ing apparently mounted to his position by way of a packing ea.e. He was dead. Witness knew him well and had also noticed his strangen--as, especially on Sun- day morning at 11 o'clock, the last time witness saw him alive. _

After a iihnrt coreiideration the jury returned a ver- dict of " Suicide whilst temporarily insane." Mr. BriAnwie mentioned that this was the third in- nn a suicide he bad attended during the day.

TRIP OF Mums. R. GARNETT AND SONS _ EMPLOYKS-

On Saturday the—e—m-p-I-o-y-e—s. of Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons, Warrington and Penketh, had their annual outing The destination was Llangollen, and the party to the number of about 100 were con- veyetl by rail in specially engaged saloon compart- ments. Favoured with lovely weather, the company took every advantage of the delightful circumst- ances in exploring the many historically interesting sights and unsurpassed grandeur of tbe'scenery with which Wales abounds. Di iving,boating on the canal, and hill climbing, occupied some pleasant hours. After tea all 4 short speeches by several of the workmen, a vote of thanks was passed to the firm for their kindness in defraying the travelling expenses. Home was reached towards :ten o'clock, all arriving in the beq of spirits, without mishap, each and all wiling the outing a huge success. The arrangements for the day were carried.' out by a committee of the workmen to the satisfaction of all.

POLO MATCH.

_ - On Tuesday night, at the Warrington Corporation Baths, a not,' match was played under the rules of the Liverpool and District Polo League between Warrington And St. Aufzustine's (Liverpool). The match was a one-sided affair, as Warrington were far more than a match for their opponents. At half-time the resul was—Warrington, 10; St. Augu-tine's, nil. After a few minutes' interval Warrington, who were defending the deep end, again played exceedingly well, goal after goal tieing scored with evident ease. Splendid passing took place. and at the conclusion of the game the score wAS as follows :—Warrington, , 15; St. Augustine's, nil. There was a large attendance,and the Warrington players were frequently applauded. Will ~lA.CIIINICS. -4GARLANIEI, ponmenget viarria,!ton. LOCAL CASES AT Lryitni.oor. SESSIONS. THREE MISCHIEVOUS BOYS BlRCHED.—lnspector Spinks informed the magistrates that on Saturday they had reported to them that a spring-cart had been stolen fnm Allen-street near the wholesale market, belonging to the executors of the late Mr. Warburton, Dallam, and con- taing a number of bundles of clover. Enquiries were made and it was ascertained that three boys were seen passing Sankey Bridges in a cart going in the direction of Widnes. They were afterwards apprehended in pos- session of the cart and brought to the police station. Their names were John Thomas Robinson, Thomas Dem- psey. and Thomas King, all 12 years of age and of the neighbourhood of Fennell-street.Robinson had committed a similar offence before.—The Chief Constable said it was one of those cases they could not pass by. Such lads had got into the habitof, when they saw a pony and cart alone, jumping in and going for 6 or 8 hours' ride.—The magistrates ordered each to receive 12 strokes of the birch. - - - • •

He went to prison. Ut;mczzLED Doos.—For allowing their dogs to he at large unmuzzled,the following were dealt with :—Robert Wilson, 102 Greenway-road Hannah Beckett, 18 Leinster Gardens ; Mary Ann Inman, Duke-st. ; Frank Richardson, 33 Cooper-st. ; and Thomas Dobson, Church-st.—A fine of 5. including costs was imposed in each case. A DOUBLE FlNF,—Ellen Gandy, St. John-st., was charged with unlawfully assaulting Ellen Pugh on the 18th inst., and further with being drunk and disorderly in St. John-street on the 24th inst.—Ellen Pugh was the only witness to prove the fret charge and stated that the defendant struck her three times in the face. She struck witness because she had been told that witness had been ques- tioning people as to the number of lodgers the defendant had.—P.C.'s Richardson and Butler proved the charge of drunkenness. She was fined ss. and costs on each charge. ASSAULTING A GlRL.—Mary Hulse, Loch-street, was summoned for having on the 22nd inst., unlawfully assaulted Sarah Annie Prire.—Mr. Allen, solicitor, appoared for the defence.—Sarah Annie Price, a girl about 14 years of age, deposed that on the night in ques- tion she was sitting at the door nursing her brother. when the defendant came up to her very drunk, and used disgraceful language. She also picked the cat up and threw it in witness's face, stating that she would do the same to her mother. She (witness) was scratched in the face by the cat.—Mrs. Price gave corroborative evidence as to the throwing of the cat in her daughter's face.—Mr. Allen stated that Mrs. Hulse was incensed against Mrs. Price for having written to her (Mrs. Hulse's) husband, He denied that she was drunk, and submitted that if she did throw the cat at the defendant it was a very trivial matter. There were no scratches on the girl's face. A witness named Dutton gave evidence for the defence.—The magistrates bound the defendant' over to be of good behaviour for six months, herself in £5, and another surety of £5, and the payment of the coats. THEFT OF BACON.—Mary Doyle, Mill-qt., was charged with stealing 31bs. of bacon, value Is. 9d., the property of William Wright.—The complainant stated that he was a pork butcher carrying on business in Church-street. About a quarter to ten on Saturday night he left the shop for a minute or two. He heard something drop.and on going into the shop Vie prisoner was there. She asked him for a quarter of a pound of Dotted meat. He told her that he had none,but she threw lid. on the counter and ran out of the shop. He at once missed the bacon and followed after her. She dropped the bacon in the street from under her shawl, and he gave her into the custody of the police. She was under the influence of Crink.— P.C. Evans stated that he saw the prisoner drop the bacon from under her shawl as Mr. Wright was following after her.—Superintendent Atherton said that prisoner had previously been a respectable woman. It was her first offence.—The magistrates hoped the case would be a warning to her and discharged her.

FRODSHAM PETTY SESSIONS.

WEDNESDAY—Before Mr. A. Thomas (chairman) and

Mr. William Mortimer. UNSEUZZLED Does.—James Edwards, Frodsham, for allowing his dog to be at large unmuzzled at Frodsham on the 27th inst was fined ss. including costs. Thomas Fletcher, Helsby, and Thomas Littler, Frodsham Lord- ship were similarly dealt with for a like offence. I AN UNLICENSED DOG.—Joseph Hopley of Helsby was summoned for keeping a dog without a license at Helsby on the 30th July.—P.C's Breeze and Wetherby stated the case for the prosecution.—DAendant contended that the dog was not six months old, hut the magistrates con- sidered it was, and fined him 23. 6d. and costs. DRUNK ON LICENSED PREMISES.—WiIIiam Gaskell. shopkeeper, Frodshain Lordship, was summoned for tie- ing drunk on the licensed premises of John A. fiate,Belle Morite, overton, on the 6.ti of August—P.O. Wetherby stated that on the date named about 4-45 he was in com- pany with P.C. Price and visited Belle Monte. The defendant was sitting in a room asleep ins drunken con- dition by himself. Witness called the attention of the landlord to him, and there was great difficulty in eject- ing him. There was drink before the defendant when they went into the room. He had been there for several hours.—P.C. Price gave corroborative evidence.—A fine of 25.6 d. and costs was imposed.

GRAPPENHALL FARISH COUNCIL.

Mr. William Long presided over a meeting of the Grap- penhall Parish Council,which was held on Monday night in the National School, Grappenliall. There were also Present—Messrs. Alan Lister Kaye, Richard Warburton, John Bradford, Thomas Borrows, Richard Boardman; Francis Ellison, and .1. S. Woodcock (clerk).—Mr. Kaye, when reporting on the proceedings of the Charity Com- mittee said that a letter had been written to the Eccles- iastical Commissioners with reference to the Bratt Charity, asking them whether the charity was eccles- iastinal or parochial. An acknowledgement of the letter had been received frcm the Commissioners, and tbev hid also replied in the lISU3I way—saying that the matter was baying attention—when asked to acquaint the Council of their opinion before this meeting. Nothing further had been heard of the matter, and the committee were consequently unable to advance until they had received a reply to their inquiry. Continuing, Mr. Kaye remarked that a letter had also been written to Mr. G. H. Linaker,Frodsham, asking by whose autho- rity the plot of land, mentioned at the Council's last meeting, had been sal, and what were the results of the sale. Mr. Linaker had not replied to that letter, bat when Mr. Kay,- met him a short time ago in a public street he said that if any member of the Council cared to go to Frodsham he would be permitted to look at his (Mr. Linaker's) books,and see the results of the sale of the land. He further said that the authority of the Local Government Board had been obtained before the land was disposed of. Under such circumstances Mr. Kaye thought the Council could gone further. Replying to the Chairman, Mr. Kaye said tt-at the words of the will concerning the BrattCharity clearly bequeathed the money "to the Parochial Schools of Grappenhall." These words seem to stamp it as a parochial charity, in which case the Parish Council had the power of controlling it.—The Chairman referred to the Johnson charity, and asked if everything had been done in reference to this charity.—Mr. Kaye replied that the committee had gone as far into the matter as possible and had found that the Council had no power over it.— Mr. Kaye reported on behalf of the committee who had been empowered to purchase a safe for the custody of the Council's books and documents, that they had not been able to fix upon one owing to a little misunderstanding which had arisen as to the day upon which the commit- tee should meet to select one. A certain day had been fixed, but Mr. Burrows had apparently mistaken that day for another, and had gone to Warrington for the purpose of joining the other members of the committee. —Mr. Burrows declared that the day upon which he went to Warrington had been definitely fixed upon in his presence, and if any alteration in the date had really been made it had been made when he was absent.— Finally Mr. Richard Warborton was added to the corn- ! mitter,and they were asked to continue their search tor I a suitable safe.

SERIOUS CERRGE -AGAINST A CROFT

MAN.

somcrroßs AT VARIANCE.

At the Warrington Occasional Court on Wednesday before Mr. James Tinsley,William Collier, farm labourer, Croft, was charged with having on the 3rd August in Croft. unlawfully and maliciously indicted grievous bodily harm upon James Weywell, with intent and malace, aforethought. Mr. J. W. Knowles, solicitor, Whines, prosecuted on behalf of Weywell, and stated that on the 3rd August Weywell was in a public-house at Croft. Collier being also there. They lived in opposite directions from the beerhnume and both left about the same time. Weywell, after a short de!av, proceeded on hi+ way home and had not got far from the public-house when he saw someone on the foetpath. This person gave him a" tup " with his elbow and knocked him off the foo,path. He was about to removstrate but before he could get the words out of his mouth he received a blow in the face which knocked him violently to the floor. The man on the footpath (whom complainant now recognised as Collier) commenced to kirk him and complainant became uncoil- PCiOUS. Abut 11 15 on that night a man of the name of Isaac Maynes was coming along the road when he saw Collier thumnin,T Weywell upon the floor and then com- mence to kick him. May nes thought prudence the better part of valour and did not interfere. A shower come on and Mavnes then moved off. During the time Collier was kicking Weywell, the latter was not Ren to attempt to defend himself. Maynes heard Collier say tt I'll pay you off for all the lot and will not stand any of your cheek any longer!' Weywell would tell them that for some years Collier had been in the habit,when they were in the same public-house, of smacking him in the face and giving him black eyes. So far back as five rears ago Collier had threated that he would "do for" Wev- well, and he had had a continuous ill•feeling towards him. Mr. Browne who appeared for the defence and had just entered the room. said : What has that to do with the present ea.e.' Mr. Knowles: A great deal, we are charging him with malice aforethought. Continuing he said that if after the evidence, the bench wet.a satisfied that Collier had laid in wait for Weywell there was proof of malice afore- thought or intent to do him grievous bodily harm. In consequence of the injuries he had received complainant had been off his work 14 working days and still felt pain in his back. . _

Mr. Browne said he had given notice that he would apply for an adjournment that day and there was nothing to be gained by goine on with the case that day. Mr. Knowles said that the notice reached him too late, and Mr. Browne had had time to get his evidence ready. There was good reason for going on RS he had all his witnesses there and was quite ready. They would want their expenses and if Mr. Browne would pay them he would consent to an adjournment but not otherwise. Mr. Browne said he had received instructions of the case too late to get it up and he had not a single witness.

.... Mr. Knowles still applied for a hearing of the case at once and Mr. H. Greenall, the magistrates' clerk, said it was the firFt time in his experence that solicitors could not come to an agreement for adjournment. _ . . •

The two Solicitors then, upon Mr. Tinsley's recom- mendation, retired for a few minutes to come to terms. On their return Mr. Browne said there was no poßsi• bility of coming to an agreement. He had never known his friend so obstinate. Mr. Browse applied for the ad- journment. Mr. Knowles said that inconvenience and expense were his objections, though he was informed th,tt the County would pay the latter. Mr. Tinsley said that the suggestion of malice afore. thought put a more serious aspect upon the charge,an4 seeing that Mr. Browne's unpreparedness woull pre• judicially affect the prisoner's case he would adjourn the hearing of the case until 2.30 on the afternoon of September 24th. This was agreed to.

Mr. Pierce Mahony, who represented North Me.tth until 1892, was chosen on Wednesday at a Parneliite Convention to contest the St. Stephen's Green division of Dublin against the newly appointed Irish Solicitor General.

A London correspondent says : I learn on the best ntnority that Mr. John Morley is at present busily en- on a history of the present century. It is intended to he a very big effort, and is expected to engage his attention for several years.

A. DISASTROUS TEIUNDER.CEAP AT

CROFT.

ONE BOY KILLED AND ANOTHER INJURED. On Tuesday afternoon about 3.20, a thunderstorm broke over the village of Croft with a fatal result. Samuel Yates, labourer, and a boy named Frederick Hayes (15), the son ot Peter Hayes, farmer, Mill- house-lane. Croft, were engaged gathering potatoes in a field off Millhouse-lane, when the storm burst. Both ran under a hedge when the rain came on,and immediately after getting to the hedge there was a vivid flash of lightning and a loud peal of thunder. Yates jumped out from the hedge, while the light- ning stpyck Hayes on the left side of the head, pas- sed do Mi the right side, and killed him instantly. Yates then ran to the lad and picked him up,finding his body burnt and cut. At the same time a boy named Sylvester was up a pear tree in the orchard of a farm close by, getting the fruit. Die sudden and loud clap of thunder so frightened him that he fell eut of the tree and frac- tured his thigh.

Definite inatructions have now been officially received at Southampton aa to the movements of the Indian war- ship Clive, which brought the Shadzada to this country, and has since been awaiting orders. She was to sail on, Thursday morning direct for Marseilles, which she will reach on September 3 or 4, and there embark the Shah- z tda. The Clive will then proceed, probably to Alexan- dria, Where the Shahzada will leave her and travel over land to Cairo, rejoining her at Suez, and then proceed 1 straight home. A correspondent of a London newspaper states : —" A friend of mine who has waited upon the I Shahzada on repeated occasions at Dorchester House, has given me some laughable accounts of his experiences. My friend's object in going was to do business, and he has taken some three thousand pounds from our dinky visitor. Before he could approach .he Prince, however, he found that palm oil on quite a royal scale was necessary. He tells me that the Shahzada is a great man at a bargain, but though he will spend hours to cheapen an article, if he really wants it he will pay for it. He has bought hundreds of watches, almost a cartload of clocks, and heaps of jewellery. When payment is made a sack of gold is dragged in by two atten- dants in good Eastern fashion, and the coins are counted out soletndly by two officials. The funniest thing my friend saw was the result of the Afghans' pathetic attempt to imitee English dress. They wear roughly-made trousers and have naked feet, but the strangest thing of all is the manner in which they deal with white shirts. These linen garments are allowed to hang a little lower than the frock coats, and the appearance of the Afghans is laughable in the extreme, and is made even more comical by their solemn faces."

LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE ASSOCIATION.

LANCASHIRE V. Yortxximut.—The annual inter-county contest at bowls was played over the private green, ' Northumberland-at., Higher Boughton, on Saturday. It may be recollected that at the opening meeting, held at 1 Mirfield, near Hudderafield, in June, the Yorkshire aide won by the heavy majority of 134 shots, previous con- tests having finished in favour of Lancashire. On Satur- • day the Torkshi e team was composed of the best I players of the county, irrespective of the districts from which they were selected,but the Lancashire aide played , but one representative from each district affiliated. Mr. Harry Simpson (Withingtoti) was appointed umpire, and was assisted by Mr. Corney (Salford) and Mr. Beaumont (Huddersfield) as reterees. In the early rounds of the event Lancashire's successes were so'numerous that hope was entertained that they would entirely extinguish the majority against them ; the home team pulled off 75 shots in about ten engagements,but that number was re- duced as the game progressed to about half. Partly re- covering tbemselvea,their favourable balance again rose to 58 by the time all the players had been disposed of. Special interest was taken in the game between ISquire the Yorkshire amateur champion,and Graham,of Hinds- ford, who two or three times in the course of the struggle took the lead from his adversary, and was eventually only distanced by the odd point. The best contested games of the day were Beard v. Westwood, Walker v. Cheetham, and Pearson v. Kirkpatrick, all even at the 20th point, and each won by the odd shot. Runaway victories for Lancashire were achieved by the representatives of North Warring- ten, Newton, Urmaton, Chorlton, Croft's Bank, Brough- ton, Woodlands, flarpurhey, and Heaton Mersey: and for Yerkshire by Huddersfield, Mirfield,and Sandal. Of the 26 renresentatives of Yorkshire who gained victories at Mirfield, eighteen dropped their colours at Higher Broughton ; and of the eight winning districts of Lan- cashire. Pendleton, Chorlton, Withington, Broughton, and Old Trafford repeated their successes. The general result of the personal successes stood—Lsacashire 19, Yorkshire 15. HONOURS FOR WARRINGTONIANS.

• In the county championship bowling match between Lancashire and Yorkshire, Warrington were represented by two players. The town's reputation in the encoun- ter was upheld by Mr. J. D. Brimelow (captain of the Warrington Cemetery team), who played Mr. H. Fleet- wood (a noted player from the Mirfleld Bowling Club), the game, after peeling at eight each, resulting in a sub- s'antial win for the IVarringtonian by nine points- -21-12. Jr will be remembered that Mr. Brimelow when at Mir&ld beat his opponent by 16 points, the game then ending 21.5. For these performances he has secured the gold medal presented by the Lancashire and Cheshire County Bowling Association for thr best average in the two encounters, his record being 42 points to 17. Mr. R. Fairbrother (Bull's Head Club) alsia took part in the match, but was not so successful as his fellow-towns- man, and had.for an opponent Mr. G. H. Stead, of Bud- der:field, the latter securing a verdict in his favour of 21-11. The gold medal has only been offered on three occasions, and this is the second time it has reached Warrington, Mr. F. BPITT, a member of the Bull's Head Club, obtaining it in 1894. Mr. Brimelow is to be com- plimented on his performance, as to secul'e the best aver- age out of a team of 34 players representing Lancashire is a splendid achievement for so young a player.

THE NEW NORTHERN FOOTBALL UNION.

The "Yorkshire Evening Post," of Wednesday night, coßtained the following

" As is already known, till whole of the Yorkshire 12 have joined the Northern Union, and the following nine Lancashire clubs, we have been given to understand, will cast in their lot :—Oldham, Wigan, Warrington, Tyldesley, Rochdale Hornets, Broughton Rangers, St. Helens. Leigh, and Widnes. In addition, Stockport (Cheshire) are said to have applied for admission ; Run- corn are believed to be probable starters ; and some Durham and Northern clubs are starred doubtful run- ners—they may or may not join. This gives a nucleus of 22 clubs to commence with-12 Yorkshire, nine Lan- cashire and one Cheshire. Obviously there are too many to permit of runnirnz the Union or the League en the competition system. :The result will be that the Yorkshire Senior Competition will be con- ducted as last year by its old management; will play for its old shield, and be run upos its old lines. Lancashire and Cheshire may be split off into a separate group—or, provided sufficient clubs join, two groups. If other counties throw in their lot, they, too, might form yet other groups, the whole governed by the old transfer laws as concerning the clubs within its organisation. Probably there will be instituted in the vague future some county championship scheme to provide the attrac- tion, which will have to be cut away by the secession from the Union, in the shape of the legitimate county championship. Possibly also, in the nebulous yet to be, there will be an Inter-county Challenge Cup contest, to fill up the vacancy left by and clash with the Yorkshire Cup. These things are in the cloud as yet, but are being mooted. Coming to the question of the Association game, the promoters of the Union think they have reason to believe that the English Association will not suspend their grounds and thus prevent them from playing under the Associa- tion code. They believe the Association to be a militant body, willing and anxious to obtain a footing in the West Riding, and they hope to be able to secure recog- nition as an independent body,rather than treatment as a band of outlaws. They may or may not be justified in this view. Again, as to the conduct of sports, from which the whole of the clubs mentioned draw a substan- tial revenue. So wholesale a stoppage of athletic gather- ings would doubtless be a serious thing for the A. A.A. and N.C.U. The former body, we stated on the author- ity of Mr. Rowland Hill, yesterday, have no arrangement with the English Rugby Union as to suspended clubs or grounds ; it is not at this precise moment known whether the N.C.U. are In a similar position. The Northern Union promoters at present feel that they might secure some differentia treatment for their players who are now amateur' runners, on the ground that they are ant professionals ipso facto, but are only paid tor broken time. But such a thing is obviously impassible—the A. 4.A. are not likely in their case to make a distinction they will not draw for the more powerful body of the Football Association. The professional footballer and the professional cricketer are alike debarred from running at amateur athletic sports, and it will probably be found that the Northern Union will have to fall back upon the expedient of run- ning sports for two classes of competitors—the very pure and the semi-professional. This may or may not he allowed, but in that direction their wishes are tend- ing. So. far, we have been unable to gather when the new Union will commence its programme of matches." I

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT

COUNCIL.

An ordinary monthly meeting of the above Council was held in Mr. Sutton's Chambers,Bewsey-st., on Wed. nesday afternoon, the Rev. G. F. Roberts presiding There were also present Messrs. William Houghton. J. Dignum, J. W. Knowles, T. Halton, J. Houghton, and J. Ball ; also Mr. S. Sutton (clerk), Mr. F. Ker- toot (nuisance inspector),Mr. William Owen (surveyor), and Dr. Sephton. (medical officer).

RE-APPOINTMENT OF OFFICERS. The Crinit, after reading the accounts for the past month, read a letter from the Local Government Board sanctioning the re-appointment of Dr.Sephton as medical officer for June 1895 and June 1896, at a salary of £100; also the re-appointment of Mr. F. lierfoot as nuisance inspector at an increased salary of £llO. THE BROOIC AT PENKETH. Correspondence was read from Captain Hughes' agent with reference to the diverting of the sewage from Pen- keth brook through a sewer which the Council proposed to cut through his land, and which stated that Captain Hughes was prepared to grant permission for this on pas„ment of £1 per annum. Mr.DIGNIIM recommended that this be allowed ,and Mr. Halton having seconded, the motion was carried. In the Nuisance Inspector's report was a complaint that the brook had overflowed its banks and filled a tank at Penketh Hall, owing to there being a flood and high tide at the same tinge.—lt was decided to let Mr. Ditchfield increase the height of the bank of the brook with clay and charge the Council.

A DANGEROD'S BRIDGE AT COLLINS GREEN. A letter was read from the clerk to the Burtonwood Parish Council dated August Ist, stating that he wa directed to ask for one or two lamps to be put under the Railway Bridge at Collins Green, which was dark and dangerous. He also directed the Council's attention to the water supply of Mr. Whitfield's cottages at Col- lins Green, complaints having been made as to its inade- quacy. After Some discussion it was decided that the Parish Council be met and the questiens considered. The CLERIC reported that the audit of accounts was finished and that the auditor reported favourably upon them.

THE SURVEYOR'S REPORT. Mr.OwEN,the surveyor reported that the drains of the houses belonging to the Colliery Company, at Collins Green, were well in hand and nearly ready for connec- tion to the main sewer, so that arrangements would shortly have to be made to deal with the sewage at the filtering tanks. He also reported that the streets Off Liverpool-read, Sankey Bridges were now ready to be handed over to the Highway Board.

===== 0002601_18950831_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
BA.NKRUPTCIES IN 1894. A GRADUAL IMPROVEMENT.

The twelfth annual report to the Board of Trade, the subject of the administration of the bankruptcy Acts for 1894, shows that the number of receiving orders made was 476. or involving liabilities £6,963,806. The assets realised £2,349,267, leaving an estimated loss sus- tained by the creditors of £5,584,173. These figures compare favourably with those of thepreceding year,tor the recdving orders are less by 112 with liabilities re- duced by £528,561, and assets less by only £469,181. The estimated loss to creditors is less by £204,763 than in 1893. The deeds of arrangement registered nambered 3,894, a deorease of 44, while the liabilities, £6,419,099, were less by 1,154,767. The estimated loss to creditors was only £4,311,316, as against £5,280,165 in the previ- ous year. The number of failures scheduled last year show a reduction amounting to nearly two per cent.,while the liabilities show a reduction of over eleven per cent. The The foregoing figures, observes the Inspector General in Bankruptcy in his report, and the fact that during the present year the failures continue to show a progressive decrease upon the figures of 1894, confirm the generally prevailing impression that the condition of business is slowly improving, and the year 1893 may probably be regarded as indicating the high water mark of insolvency over a considerable period. With regard to the chief causes of insolvency the " Insprctor-General remarks : —Among the trading failures these may be summarised as follows—a lack of business , capital ; ignorance of book-keeping or want of attention to books and accounts; excessive trade credit given to i untrustworthy customers; misplaced confidence in employees either at home or abroad ; the system of accommodation bills ; reckless speculation in produce, carried on when the debtor knows himself to be insolv- ent. In the financial and speculative failures, apart from ordinary trading, the chief features appear to be those which arise from the temptations afforded to ingen- ious persons by the state of the law relating to joint stock companies for the formation of companies which do not meet, and are frequently, not intended to meet, any real public requirements,and the numerous breaches of trust which frequently accompany failures of this class. In cases of gambling and personal extravagance, Am., the chief sufferers are the unfortunate debtors them- selves. In the case of speculative adventurers, more especially where the speculation is carried on by meana of company promotion, the main result, so far as the public is concerned, has been to throw considerable di:- credit upon the latter form of enterprise. The injurious effects of trading failures are wider in their range, although perhaps not so fully appreciated or understood. The point which the Inspector-General emphasises is that where a debtor has once realised that his capital is lost, and that he is trading entirely at the risk of his creditors any deliberate attempt to carry on his business by sel- ling goods at a loss for the sake of keeping himself afloat, is and ought to be recognised as far more injurious to the commercial interests of the trading community as a whole than some even of the specific bankruptcy offences which are dealt with under the criminal law.

INSPECTOR ISIcATEER, Warrington, August 22n1, 1895.

-- THE PECULIAR PEOPLE. Sir,—Under this heading .The Manchester Guardian" *f last Friday reported that William Cable, a farm labourer, of Rayleigh, had been committed for trial at the next Essex Assizes on the charge of cruelty to his Children inasmuch as he had omitted to call in a doctor when they were suffering from diphtheria. It was pleaded in his defence that he had violated no law ; that there was no statute which compelled a man to have medical assistance, and that he bad used the remedies resorted to by the sect of which he is a member. But it WU all in vain; the law must take its course. My reader, does it not strike you as a remarkable circumstance that such a prosecution is possible in this nineteenth century of the Christian dispensation, and in a country, too, where many millions a year are paid in salaries to men who profess to be sent from Ged to preach Christianity ! In anointing the bodies of the children with oil and praying ever them for their re- sovery, the Essex labourer and his friends were not fol- lowing a cunningly devised fable, nor even acting upon the prescription of a quack doctor. Their authority was no other than the Apoole James. That there may be no mistake about it, I will quote the Apostle's words. Ad- dressing himself to Christians, and to Christians only,he MS :—" Is any sick among you : let him call for the eiders of the Church : and let them pray over him anointing him with oil in the name of the Lord : and the prayer of faith shall save the sic and the Lord shall raise him up." (Jmnes V. 14, 15). And yet for his obedience to this explicit and unmistakable teaching poor Cable must be p _wished ! I know full well that the men who pose as the spiritual guides of the people have turned the divinely appointed and suggestive rite of Baptism into the hollow sham of baby sprinkling, on the ground that it would be a dangerous thing to immerse asen's bodies in cold water in this variable climate of curs ! But I have yet ':0 learn why they quietly set aside the Apostle's instructions for the treatment of the sick ? Is it because their confilence rests more in the doctor and his bottles of medicine than in the power of the Divine healer in whem they profess to believe! There are some shining lights in the pulpits of Warrington who could, no doubt, enlighten our ignorance on this very important point. May I venture to hope that they will graciously condescend to favour us with the desired information ?—Yours truly, lONA.

DARESBURY.

- - SCHOLARS' TRRAT AT DARESBURY.—The scholars at- tending the Board School, numbering about 240, have bad their annual tea party, provided by the proceeds of a concert in February last. A field for sports and amuse- ments was kindly placed at their disposal by lair. Billing- bon, and at four o'clock an excellent tea was provided in like schoolroom. At six o'clock the Walton band com- menced to play selections, and dancing was indulged in by many friends of the scholars until dusk. The treat seemed to be thoroughly enjoyed by all, and as the juveniles left the field a packet of sweets and a bun were given to each.

FRODSHAM.

Licssisixo SESSIONI3.—These sessions were held at I Frodsham Police Court on Wednesday morning. The magistrates prerent were Mr. A. Thomas and Mr. W. Mortimer.—Superintendent Johnson read his annual re- port with refetence to the conduct of licensed victuallsrs and beer retailers in the division during the past year. He said there were 17 licensed victuallers, 8 beersellers, and one other who sold off the premises, total 26 ; giving an average of 258 of the population to each license. One licensed victualler had been proceeded against during the year, ens of whom was convicted,being, an increase of one pro- ceeded against and one convicted in the preceding year. Two beersellers had been proceeded against and one con- victed, being an increase of one proceeded against and convicted in the preceding year. Thirty-seven persons had been'proceeded against for drunkenness during the year, 37 of whom were convicted as compared with 46 persons proceeded against .and 45 convicted during the preceding year, being a decrease of nine persons pro- ceeded against.—lt Was BU-fed that he licensee of the 'Robin Hood," Helsby, hadtied, and that a transfer of the license would be applied for at the adjourned sessions en the 30th September.—Uere were no new applications, and no objections were raised to the renewal of the exist- ing licenses.—The magistrates accord' ngly renewed the licenses. -. •

WARRINGTON ANGLERS ASSOCIATION. SONS OF TEMPERANCE.

The usual meeting in connection with the Samuel Morley Section of Cadets of Temperance was held at the Co-operative Hall, Earlestown, on Wednesday evening at 7 o'clock, Bro. A. Staith in the chair. At eight o'clock the Adult Division met in the same room and Bro. John Pickett presided. After the opening, ceremony and other routine busi- ness was gone throu,gh,the subject of the anniver- sary came up and after a discussion on the subject it was resolved that they celebrate the anniversary with a tea. A sub-committee was appointed to make all necessary arrangements for the same and the meeting closed at 9.30.

A meeting was held in the Co-operative Build- ings, on Tuesday evening, for the purpose of con- sidering the rights of Common in this neighbour- hood. Although it was urged that the recent Local Government Act conferred full powers upon the District and County Councils, to deal witn.,such matters, it was nevertheless deemed adviiable to form a Local Commons Preservation Society, which was accordingly done, its subjects being to make note of all road-side wastes within the district, and also to aid the District Council in its endeavour to prevent the erection of houses on Newton Common, in order that the land may be preserved fur pur- poses of public recreation. EARLESTOWN FOOTBALL CLUB.

1 The football season at Earlestown opens on Sat- urday next week, the 7th September, when the Earlestown club team journey to Birmingham ti.. play the Small Heat!' reserve team. The following Saturday they oppose Ashton-in-Makeifield in the qualifying round of the Lancashire junior cup com- petition, and the result of this march will decide whet her our local team is debarred from any further participation in the competition or not. The cons-, position of the Earlestown team remains the same as it did during last season, all the old players having signed again. This will doubtless be a dis- tinct advantage to the club, as it will take very little practice indeed to put the members of the team in touch with each other's play, seeing that they fought side by side last year. It is expected that under these fortunate circumstances the team will render a good account of itself, and improve somewhat on last year's record, which, taken as a whole, was fairly successful. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 31, 1895

WEDNESDAY. The House of Commons !lilt on Wednesdlr at noon and immediately went into Committee of Supply on the Civil SPrvi e Eatimates. On the Education vote (£3,335,485) for England and Wales, Mr. Erman' GRAY urged the total abolition of the system of payment by .results, and suggested that steps should be taken to meet the distressing condition of many voluntary schools. Mr.Yoxam. (in a maiden speech) expressed the opinion that further pecuniary aid was required for public ele- mentary schools in England and Wales—not only in the case of voluntary schools, but of many Board schools. One of the most efficient means of givinr, aid to schools would be to establish a pension system, which wuld enable old and worn out teachers to retire and be re- placed by younger and more competent teachers. Mr. DILL,N called attention to the grievances of Roman Catholic voluntary schoots,more particularly in regard to the stringent demands which had recently been made in respect of structural changes. Mr. T. P. O'Corroon referred to the case of the Catholics in his own consti- tuency, who held that they had a grievance in that they not only had to pay for their own schools, but also to subscribe to a considerable School Board rate for the edu- cation of the children of parents of other religious creeds. While they subscribed to the public rates, the rates in no way helped them in the maintenance of their own schools. Sir J. GORST promised to consider the consolidation and reform of the laws relating to the age at which children could be employed and also the half-time sys- tem. The question of teachers' pensions had passed be- yond the stage of controversy, and a scheme had been elaborated for dealing with it. The teachers might feel confident that next session a measure would be laid on the table differing in no essential particular from that prepared by the late Government. The policy of the Government towards voluntary s,bools was an impor- tant matter, and if he were quesloned on the subject next session he had no doubt that he should be able to give a satisfactory answer.

Afterwards, on a vote of £7 for the University of London, Dr. TANNER came into conflict with the e hair- man, who threatened to deal with the member for Mid- Cork if he persisted in obstructing business—a warning to which Mr. T. M. HEALY retorted by saying" Suspend us all at once." Some time afterwards the closure was applied, and the vote carried. THURSDAY. Mr. Pownt, WILLIAMS stated in the House of Com- mons on Thursday night, in reply to Mr. Weir, that ar- rangements for the manufacture of cordite had been made with Messrs. firmness and Ce'' ' . of Birmiugham and the National Explosives Company, London. Since 1889 a sum of £47,750 had been spent in the ordance fadthries on machines for the manufacture of nitro-glycerine! and cordite, but no money had been given to any private firm for the same purpose. Mr. CURZON informed Mr. Cmastsuma that so informa- tion had reached the Government as to the threatening attitude of the Turkish troops towards the Christian population in the neighbourhood of Erzinghian, in Armenia, or as to any attacks during the last few days on villages and monasteries. Replying to Sir E. A. Rairrurrr, Mr. CURZON said the questions raised by the conflict between French troops and a British force at Weima in December, 1893, by which three British officers lost their lives, had been included in the general negotiations with France which were suspended under the late Administration. The geographical position of Weima had not been fixed, and it was not known in whose territory it is. Asked by Mr. LEWIS whether the Government in- tended to take any practical steps to obviate distress arising from want of employment during the coming winter, Mr. CHAPLIN sail the Government fully appre- ciated the importance of the question, and they would watch very closely any increase of destitution due to want of employment, with (wry desire to render such assistance as circumstances might admit.

CYCLE ACCIDENT AT EARLESTOWN. On Wednesday afternoon a bicycle accident happened in Wargrave-road,Earlestown,but fortun- ately it was not of a very serious nature. A lorry belonging to the London and North-Western Com- pany was going up Wargrave-road, when two cyc- lists passed on the same side going in opposite directions. They evidently had not seen each other until too late, as they collided with much force, the machine of one, a youth named Sutton, being smashed, and the rider thrown and shaken. The other cyclist was apparently unhurt. EXCURSION FROM COLLINS GREEN.

The annuai excUrsiciii-prgmoted by the employes of the Collins Green Colliery Co., took place on Sat urday to Blackpool. Two trains carrying about 1,500 people left Collins Green shortly after six o'clock,and Blackpool was reached without mishap. The weather was beautiful throughout the day and the outing was thoroughly enjoyed by those who accompanied the trip The return journey was made at different times, the last train reaching Col- lins Green about two o'clock on Sunday morning.

In the House of Commons on Monday Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN informed Mr. Johnston that the negotiations be- t Arlen Newfoundland and Canada with regard to the incorporation of the former with the Dominion were I now in abeyance, he believed owing to the disinclination Of the Dominion to take over the very large debt of the colony. HAMILTON said he had received a memorial from representatives of the various cotton industries in Lancashire stating fully and clearly their ohj-ctions to the present excise duty levied on cotton yarns in India. The memorial would be sent to the Indian Government at once,with a request for a state- ment of their side of the case. He adhered to the speech he made in February, and had nothing to retract. Mr. FLYNN asked Lord George Hamilton if he was aware that a large number, if not all,of the Lancashire Conser- vative members were returned on the strength of the re- opal of the cotton duties. In the House or Lords, Lord LANSDOWNffi made a state- ment with regard to the intentions of the Government as to the new military arrangements. tnstead of appoint- ing a Chief of the Staff, as recommended by the Harting- ton Commission, they were appointing a Commander-in- Chief, who would exercise gimeral control over her Majesty's forces at home and abroad, would issue Army orders and hold periodical inSp.ctions, would be respon- sible for commissions, promotions, appointments, honours, and rewards, and also, with the assistance of two high military officers, for the departments of mili- tary information and mobilisation. His lordship pro- ceeded to define the duties of the heads of the various departments, and stated that these officers,togpther with the Conintander-io-Chief, would form a consultative hoard, hit' the Secretary of State alone would be respon- sible to Parliament. TUESDAY.

In the Hons.- of Commons on Tuesday Mr. BCC.IIANAN asked tlif, Secretary for India whether, in view of the decided expression n the opinion of the House of Com- mons on the 14 of February on the subject of the Indian cotton dutiPs,he would promise that the duties should not be repealed until the House had again had an op- portunity of expressing its opinion on the subject. Lord G. Flamwros replied that nothing in connection with the duties would be done either hastily or surrepti- tiausly. - Questioned as to the hanging of a British trader named Stokes by Belgian officers in Central Africa, Mr. CulizoN said the Government had not yet received any reply from the Belgian Government to the inquiries ad- liressivi to them as to the execution of Stokes. The Government regarded the cage as one of great import- ance. and were pressing for an early answer. On the House going into Committee of Supply the con- sideration of the Civil Service Estimates was resumed. On the vote for the salary of the Chief Secretary for Ire- land, Mr. T. M. Healy said it was beyond question that some relief was desirable and was expected by the ten- ant farmers of Ireland. A Bill was abmt to be brem,ht in to renew that section of the Act of 1891 which referred to evicted tenants, and he suggested that the section of the Act of 1887 which gave the Land Com- mission Judges power by a sliding scale to vary judicial rents should also be renewed. He also urged that the Judges should be prepared to give concessions in the Land judges Cpurt whether the rents in question were judicial rents or not. Mr. G. BALFOUR, in reply. said that though the policy of the Government was one of infif4ible opposition to Home Rule, it was not one of inflexible opposition to the desires of the Irish people. It had been said that by a stroke of the pen he could re- instate the evicted tenants, but it was quite clear that neither he nor ass- other Chief Secretary could possibly do that. It was his hops that the Governmentwould be able in the present session to reenact the 13th clause of the Act of nu, but he thought that no such urgent condition of affairs • had Arisen se would justify the Government in bringing fotward a B'll for the revision of judicial rents. As to the proposal to give the Land Court power Co apply a sliding scab so as to vary rents according to prices, it could not fail to be contentious in the highest degree, and it would therefore be useless to consider the desirableness of bringing forward such a measure.

SHIP CANAL.

COLLISION AT SALTPORT.

On Monday morning a collision occurred on the Ship Canal at Saltport. A barque named the " Southern Queen" was moored there and had almost finiihed t tk- ing in bal!a-t for departure when she was struck on her port bow by the steamer "Clan Murray ",which was passing down the canal. The barque broke away from her mooring, smashed her bulwark and rail and alsa damaged some of the channel plates on the starboard side. Through the wash caused by the "Clan Murray" the " Ptarmigan," which was also at Saltport," was carried front her moorings. LAST WEEK'S CANAL TRAFFIC. During the seven days ended Friday week last 145 sea goine vessels, with a total net registered tonnage of 38,995 tons, made use of the Ship Canal ; 78 vessels, with a tonnage of 20,449 tons entered ; whilst 67 steam- ers and sailing ships,representing a net tonnage of 18,546 tons, cleared outwards. Of the arrivals 65 were trom destinations in the Matted Kingdom and 13 from foreign ports ; 13 vessels sailed to places abroad, the other 52 departures being from home destinations. Among the imports were full cargoes of iron and steel,timber,slates, stone, China clay, logwood and pig iron, the remaining vessels bringing, with one or two exceptions, mixed cargoes, and a large number of full cargoes of coal and salt were taken outwards. The proportion of vessels arriving or departing light was small. These figures in- clude the traffic at all points of the canal. THE IMPORTATION OF DRIED FRUIT.

Owing to the comparatively large—n—tnnt;;-Of vessels which are now lying in the docks at the Manchester end of the Ship Canal the neighbourhood has a very busy appearance,as with very few exception's all the ships are either receiving or discharging cargo. The steamer Ocean Prince on Wednesday arrived in the can. with a large quantity of Valencia raisins on board for Manchea• ter. This is the first consignment of raisins from Spain that has been received direct this season, and is con- signed to the Ship Canal Importing Company, which has also made arrangements for a direct line of sailings between Manchester and the Grecian ports during the dry fruit season, the steamer Borghese being at present loaded at Patras, from which port she will arrive here about the third week in September. Four other cargoes have also been arranged for, and in order to develop the the traffic specialists from Liverpool and London will have charge of the weighing and sampling, so that at Manchester the despatch will in no wise be lower thanat other ports.
