saociitNu ACCIDENT TO A MARINE FIREMAN AT

SALTPORT.

Mr. Sampson,coroner, held an inquest on Wednesday, at Livernool,into the circumstances under which Arthur Breen, 27, a marine fireman, met his death. Breen was a native of the county Armagh, and had been employed on board the, steamship Ptarmigan,now on her voyage to Rotterdam. On Sunday evening, while the vessel was lying at Ssltport,Breen and three ether men asked John Rawlinson,the master of a flat that lay close by, to show them the way to Frodsham. Suspecting that they wanted to get to a public-house, Rawlinson tried to persuade them to remain on board their vessel. Next morning Rawlinson rose about five o'clock, and on going on deck he found Breen I lying on the deck bleeding from the mouth and unconscious. Be thought that Breen must have fallen from the quay to the deck of the flat, a distance of ten or twelve feet. Mr. William Shaw, agent at Runcorn for the Mersey Mission to Seamen, stated that on Monday evening he had been told that a fireman bad been badly hurt at altport. He went there and found the man lying, still unconscious, on a hand ambulance. The captain of the vessel declined to take the man on baard as the ves- sel was ready to go to sea. Witness then had Breen con- veyed to the Royal Infirmary, Liverpool, where he died the same day, fracture of the skull being the direct cause. The Coroner said Mr. Saw had acted in a very humane and praiseworthy manner, and that his action showed the value cf such societies as the Mersey Mission to Seamen. If the jury wished he would adjourn the in- quest for six weeks in order to bring the captain of the steamer before them. The jury thought that course un- necessary, and found a verdict to the eff6ct that the de- -1 ceased died from fracture of the skull, but as to how the injury was sustained they had no evidence to show.

CLAIM BY LOCAL FURNITURE DEALERS.

At the Warrington County Court on _Thursday morning, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, Messrs. G. and J. Weaver. furniture dealers, of Bridge-street, Warrington, sought to recover from William Bate, gas stoker, of byrnm, the sum of £1 10A. 61, representing the balance of an account entered into by the defendant with the plaintiffs for certain furniture supplied,—Mr. Browne appeared for the plain, iffs, and Mr. Fowler for the defendant.—George Weaver said that goods had been sold to the defendant in 1888. He produced his books showing the debt, and also showing that 3s. had been paid on the 19th November, 1889, and 2s. 61. on the let April, 1890. Ilia balance against the defendant. was also set forth in the books.— James Weaver stated that on the Ist April he was Paid 2s. 64. by the defendant's wife, who was then in kyrnm.—William Bate, defendant, denied having sent any postage stamps ; nor bad he paid anything since 30th April, 1888, when a balance. of £1 16i. was owing. No one from the plaintiff's had called upon him since that time; indeed since that time he had not hoard anything about the account.—Cross- examined by Mr. Browne,defendant still adhered to his statement. His wife had made no payments, or she would have informed him of the fact.—Defend- ant's wife was called, and bore out her husband's statement. She had not made any payment since the 30th April, 1888. She had never had a receipt from the plaintiff, James Weaver, as he had stated. —The Judge said he could not reject the evidence of the two plaintiffs, who showed that payments had been regularly entered into their books as late as April, 1890. The only conclusion he could come to was that Mrs. Bate mu,t have forgotton that she had paid 24. Gd. in April, 1890. It was quite possiLle that she hail forgetton tiese payments. He must, therefore, give judgment for plaintiffs for the full amount claimed.

NEWTON DISTRICT COUNCIL.

SPECIAL MEETING.

On Wednesday afternoon a special meeting of the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Ceuucil was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, Earleatown. The meeting was called to take into consideration the rejection of the minutes, at the last meeting, of the let July. There were present Mr. C. B. F. Barron (chair- man), and Messrs. Collingwood, Slee, Stone. Thompson, jun., Simpson, MacMahon, Gilmour, T. Ormand, the , Rev. E. W. W. Kaye, Mr. J. E. Worsley (law clerk), and Mr. R. Brierley (clerk and surveyor). After the Law Clerk had read the minutes of the last meeting, Mr, Orrnand asked on whose authority the meeting had been called. The Law Clerk informed the Council that it had been called upon the requisition signed and sent him by the chairman in accordance with No. 4of the Standing Orders.

Mr. MacMahon asked if it was necessary that that meeting should have been called seeing .that the Council met again on Monday. The Chairman simply referred him to the Standing Orders.

Mr. MacMahon (continuing), said he could not for the life of him, see any necessity for the meeting. Be had read the Standing Orders and understood them but he still could see no need for a special meeting. The Chairman said that the meeting had been called in order to progress with the business of the Council and it was in the interests of the ratepayers that sucb should be done. (Mr. Gilmour : Question, question.) Mr. MacMahon still protested against " this unneces- sary meeting," as he termed it, saying that members were called upon to give their time come a week to com- mittee meetings and once a month to Council meetings and he thought that during that time they represented tile intereqtq of the ratepayers quite sufficiently. Eventually the Chairman ruled him out of order.

The Chairman then moved the following resolution "That the resolution of the Council rejecting the reso- lution that the minutes of the last monthly meeting ot the Council (Ist July, 1895) be signed, come to at the last monthly meeting of the Council of the 12th of August instant, be and is hereby rescinded." He said that owing to circumstances which had arisen, and which had prevented the minutes ot the Council meeting of the Ist July being passed, the motion was nec-ssary. He had already stated that such a course was in accordance with standing order No. 4, and he hoped that the Council would unanimously recognise the minutes. The meeting was necessary owing to the extraordinary circumstances due to the rejection of the minutes, and he therefore, without further remark, moved the resolution. Mr. Thompson seconded. Mr. Gilmour thought they ought to discuss the cause of the rejection of the minutes. They were advocating the interests of the ratepayers generally, and they ought not to sit down if the minutes were tampered with. If anyone was to blame in the matter it was the chairman, for if he had put the minutes to the •-neeting with the ,ex- ception of the one relating to the appointment of over- seers they would have been passed. Be had no objection to the other portion of the minutes being passed. As to the present meeting he could not see any necessity for it and he complained of the amount of time which mem- bers were asked to" waste." He represented the inter- ests of the ratepayers as well as any other member of the Council and the ratepayers believed it. Mr. Ormand asked if the minutes of the let of July had been corrected and the chairman replied in the negative.

Mr.Ormand said that in that case he could not support the resolution. The Law Clerk said it was not his intentien in the least to misrepresent the views of any member of the Council, and be asked that in future a change should be adopted. At present he prepared a summary of what transpired. That was very easy so long as the meetings lasted only a short time, but when meetings lasted as three had recently been doing, it was an utter impossi- bility for any person to hold the balance fairly, and he contended that no person held it more fairly than he had done. He asked them in future to instruct him that he should only record resolutions with a small introduc- tion to each instead of giving a summary of each speaker's views. Such a course, he thought, would ob- viate a great deal of trouble. At the request of the Chairman he then read the whole of the minutes from the Ist July. Mr. Ormand, after thanking the Chairman and the Law Clerk for the reading of the minutes, said he must take exception to them. If they had been amended he would have supported their adoption, but as they had not been he had no alternative but to vote against them. They had no record on the minutes as to how many over- seers they had the power to appoint. They were still guided by the Law Clerk's advice that they could only appoint not more than two although the circular issued by the Local Government Board stated that they could appoint two but not more than four. That circular, the Law Clerk hal confirmed but not on the minutes. Referring to Standing Order No. 4, he said that it meant net only that the minutes should be read and signed by the Chairman, but that they should also be corrected. The Law Clerk said that he had stated at the maeting of the Ist July his reasons for advising them to appoint not more than two. He asked Mr. Ormand if it would meet his views if he altered the minutes as follows :—After the words " The Law Clerk explain. d the matter to the Council and stated his reasons for the advice given by him "to add the fallowing words" and stated that that advice referred to the first appoint- ment." Mr. Ormand said that he would be satisfied if it were recorded co the minutes that they had the power to I appoint from two to four overseers. The Chairman upheld the Law Clerk in his views say- ing that he had advised them (the Council) very judici- ously to appoint not more than two which was in har- mony with the circular issued by the Local Government Board. The Law Clerk had,he said,explainel that Urhan Councils and other like authorities could appoint two, three, or four. The minutes were therefore a correct record of the proceedings on the Ist July. The Law Clerk then read an extract from the cireul rr of the Local Government Board relative to the appoint- ment of overseers. This stated that it would seem desirable that under ordinary circumstances the same number of overseers should be appointed, but that the Council could if they thought fit replace them by church- wardens. It could appoint no less than 2 and not more than 4. The speaker then went on to say that in Newton no churchwardens had ever acted and he therefore thought it wise to advise the Council to appoint the same number as heretofore-2. That was in order to keep the Council safe as to the appointment. Messra. Stone and Thompson having supported the resolution it was put to the meeting and carried. Those voting for it were the Chairman, Messrs. Coiling wood, Simpson. Stone, Thompson, Slee,and the Rev. E. W. W. Kaye. Those voting against were Messrs. Manlahon, Gilmour, and Ormand. The Chairman then moved "that the said minutes of the said monthly meeting of the Council of the let July, 1895, be signed, and incorporated with the proceedings of the Council."

Mr. Simpson seconded. Mr. Ormand moved an amendment to the effect that the Council accept the Law Clerk's explanation and the reason for his recommending the Council to appoint not more than 2 overseers, but that it be recorded on the minutes that in future the Council have authority to appoint 2,3, or 4 overseers. Mr. Gilmour seconded the amendment, and said that at the last meeting when he put the question the Law Clerk had said distinctly that they could not appoint more than two. Ile gave it as his opinion that the Law Clerk had mislead the Council. Mr. MacMahon supported the amendment, and made similar assertions. Mr. Thompson, while strongly objecting to any Altera- tion of the minutes, thought the amendment was neces- sary for the future of the Council. • Mr. Simpson recommended immediate voting, and said they could not alter the minutes for it was the reso- luti An which the meeting were called upon to consider. The Chairman then put the amendment and those for it were Messrs. MacMahon, Gilmour, and Ormand. Against it were the Chairman and Messrs. Collingwood, Stene, S'mpson, Thompson, Slee, and the Rev. -E. W. W. Kaye. . _ .

• '• The re-solution was then formally put to the meeting and passed. This concludod the proceedings. --- BUSINESS OF THE NEXT MEETING. A meeting( of this Council will be held on Monday at the Town Hall, Elrlestown, when the following minutes of committees will be taken into consideration :

Mr. Webb intimated auy,additional cost for improved 'supply must be borne by the Council." The following is toe correspondence with Mr. Webb : Mr. Webb wrote to the District Council on March 20, as follows : "Gentlemen,-1 got authority some time ago to lay a new main between our Parkside Pumping Station and Newton le-Willows, so as to give a better supply of water than we have hitherto been able to do,but I have heard, incidentally, that you are looking out elsewhere for a supply for the town. Before we incur the contemplated expense, I shall be glad to hear from you whether this is so, as if you propose to leave us' the proposed expendi- ture will be unnecessary. Your early reply will obliee." Oho May 14, Mr. Brierley replied on behalf of the Dis- trict Council AR follows:—" Dear Sir,—Your comrnuni- c tion of 20. h March last has been further considered by a Special Committee, who desire an interview with you, and .is they understand you are frequently in this neigh- ourlood,l am to ask you to name a day and time when they could meet you at Newton or Earlestown." Ou May 23, Mr. Webb replied :—"Dear Sir,—ln reply to your letter of the 14th inst., I shall be at Earlestown Works on the 1411 proximo, and could see your Commit- , tee at Mr. Emmett's Office, at 10.30 a.m. on that day, but it they want an interview with the object of obtain- ing lower terms for the water, it will be no use their seeing moon the subject, as we are buying water our- selves, and can very well make use of ail the water available at Parkside for our own purposes. My object in writing to you was to ascertain whether the rumour that I had he ird, that you were thinking of obtaining water elsewhere, was correct, as in that case, we should not put in the new main from Parkside to Newton,which we had proposed to do, in order to give you a better preasure, and the pipes for which had already been ordered, and are on hand." Mr. Brierley again wrote on the Bth June:—" Dear Sir,—l am interacted to inform you in reply to your letter of the 22nd ult., that the Sub-Committee regret that you are not able to see your way to grant them an interview to discuss fully the terms and the questions raised by you, regarding the present and future supply of water for thia district. I may state that prior to your letter of 20th March last, no discussion nor any steps had been taken by the Council as to any change in filo existing arrangements with your Company. The Sub-Committee will now be glad to know if in your contemplated additions, whether the pumping plant is or will he so far duplicated throughout, that a constant supply of water will be maintained to the district in case o a breakdown or stoppage on any part of the plant ? If you are prepared to maintain a constant pressure of not less than 30Ihs. to the square inch at the points of deli very into our mains at railway level ? Whether pro- vision has or will be made for intercepting by settling or filtrating tanks, the sand which is now sent in exces- sive and obj -ctionable quantities into our mains? Whether any and what reserve of water (covered in) will be made,beyond the present tank at Earlestewn, as a provision in case of fire or stoppage of all or part of the pumping plant, so as to ensure an ample supply of water to the district in case of such emergencies. lam desired to mention that the Sub-Committee are of opinion that the Council will endeavour to act in har- mony with so large a ratepayer as your company, and if placed in a fair and reasonable position would deprecate any action which might ultimately tend to permanently injure the company's smiree of water supply." On June 12;h Mr. Webb wrote :—" Dear sir,—Advert- ng to your letter of the 10th inst. I shall have my 'committee at Earlestoivn on Friday. and if your sub- committee wish to see them on the water question, I would recommend them to be there at 12 o'clock, and enquire fir me at Mr. Emmett's office." On Jose 13ih, Mr. Brierlev wrote :—" Dear sir,—l beg to acknowledge yours of yesterday's date and regret I am unable on so short notice to arrange a meeting, owing to one of the principal members of the sub-com- mittee being in London ; if I may suggest it, I think if von will kindly answer the queries in my letter of the 10' lo inst., it would facilitate businers. Our sub-corn- runic) could then consider y our reply and perhaps an interview would bei unnecessary ; on the other hand, I shall he pleased to call a meeting of our committee at the earliest moment for an intervievv7if you think it desir- able. Awaiting your reply." The final letter dated 17th June, received from Mr. i Webb on June 17 h, was as follows :—" Dear sir,—With regard to yours of the 13'h inst. If you will refer to our , correspondence you will find that my letter of the 12th inst., "Minh you say is short notice, was merely a re- minder of the one wrote to you on May 23rd, giving the same day for the proposed meeting, only altering the hour slightly ; but as you were not there I laid the papers before my committee,and I was instructed to in- form you that we cannot guarantee a constant pressure of 30ihs. on the square inch, as you request, and Ido not think you would find any Water Company prepared to do so ; any increased storage required for the benefit of the town.woirld have to be provided by the authori- ties of the town itself, and at their own cost ; neither can we make a-. 17 alteration in the present terms upon which the water:. is supplied, but if you can find a better ' aotirrle of supply for yourselves it will make no difference to us if you cease taking water, as we can readily age it for our own re quiremente, instead of purchasing else- where, as we have to do occasionally." TEE BURIAL GROUND QUESTION AT BURTON WOOD.

An adjourned meeting of the ratepayers of the parisa of Burtonwood was held in the Board School on Thurs- day evening, for the purpose of further considering the question of additional burial ground accommodation Mr.A.Jackson (chairman of the Parish Council,presidsd . and the other members of the Council present were Messrs. W. Senhton,T. Belltield, W. Southern,J.Warbur- ton, and W. Fairclough, besides whom there were pre- sent representatives of all religious denominations. Mr. John Forster (assistant overseer) read the notice convening the meeting, and also the minutes of the last meeting held on May 30th, when there was a tie in the number of votes for a cemetery, and the question was left over. The minutes were confirmed on the motion , of Mr. Southern, seconded by Mr. Warburton. _ .

The Chairman, said he was sorry that the Vicar was not present that night, and his absence would show that he would fall in with the views of the parishioners. The question was a very serious one for the parishioners of liurtonwood. The old burial ground was quite full up, and anyone needing a grave must go up and down the graveyard seeking in every corner for a spot. He thought the parishioners should take the matter up without de- lay, and hoped that they would do their best, and if the parsh meeting would consent for the Parish Council to take the matter up he had no doubt they would go into it fully and ventilate it. With regard to the.cost, in the Act it tol4 them they could borrow money on the rates to the extent of sixpence in the gound,aud no more than that in any one year, and that would extend ever a period of somithing like 60 years,so that it would not be the present generation which would have to bear the ex- pense. Mr. W. Southern moved that they adopt the burials Act, and that they leave the question of the burial ground in the hands of the Parish Council, who would do their heat to try and work the thing through. They knew exactly what the parish wanted. It was import- ant not only to church people, but to Non-conformists, Catholics and everybody to have a burial ground in Bur- tonwood, instead of going to Sutton, St. Helens, Earles- town and Newton. rhey wanted a burial ground that they could go to at the last moment, when they had anyone to bury. He thought it was a downright shame that they had only got a church place to go and bury in, and he thought if they would only leave it with the Parish Council, they would do well. (Hear, hoar.)

Mr. J. Warburt9n endorsed all that Mr. Southern had said. The question had been on for some years, and he wanted an additional ground making at the time he was churchwarden, when it could have been done at very little expense. He thought it was time they found somewhere to bury the dead and it was nothing but right that all denominations should be buried in one place. He seconded the motion.

• Mr. W. Sephton supported, and said he did not see the necessity for any discussion. It was a disgrace to the pari,h te be without a burial ground. It was simply a question of adopting the Act and carrying it out, and with regard'to the-cost, about £1,500 would pay for all. A new site for a burial ground was required, and they should get to work as soon as they could. Mr. F. Davies also suppotted and said it was a wise suggestion as they had found the Parish CounciVad done their best, and spent their time in the interests of the parishioners. The motion was put to the meeting, and unanimously carried.

RESIGNATION OF THE CHIEF CONSTABLE. At a meeting of the Warrington Watch Com- mittee which was held on Thursday week, a letter was received from Mr. Samuel Hunt, the chief con- stable, resigning his position as chief constable of the borough of Warrington on the grounds of ill- health, and advancing years. The committee accepted the resignation at the same time express- ing their great regret that Mr. Hunt was about to retire from the public position which he has held so long and so successfully. The committee decided to advertise for a successor at a salary of £.300 per annum, which is the salary that Mr. Hunt at present receives. Mr. Hunt will continue to perform the duties of chief constable until the ap- pointment of his successor. It may be mentioned that Mr. Hunt came from Derby to Warrington so long ago as September 21st, 1863, to act as superim• tendent. On August 9th, 1866, he was appointed chief constable of the borough, a position which he has held ever since. It is expected that in recog- nition of his long services to the town,a testimonial will be raised, and it will no doubt receive a very hearty response. Mr. Bunt will, of course, owing to his long service in the police force be entitled to a substantial pension. • WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON. Week ending 17th August,lB9s. During the week, which was the 33r1 of the current year, the following births and deaths were registered : Births. Deaths Warrington 3B 19 Latchford 5 Total 43 19 The excess of births over deaths was 24. The death rate from all causes was equal to 17.3 per 1,000, against 13.4 the average of the previous four weeks, and against 13.9 the corresponding week of last year. The death- rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 1.8 per 1,000, against 3.1 the average rate of the four previous weeks, and against 1.8 the corresponding week 0f,1894. The ages of mortality were- -0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65 up. 6 5 1 2 3 2 19 19 The deaths included-2 from diarhcea, 2 from phthisis 1 from premature birth, 1 from old age, 2 from lung disease, 3 from brain disease, 1 from burns. The following cases of infectious disease have been reported :during the week :—Erysipelas, 1 case, Scarlet fever, 5 cases, enteric fever 1 case. Total number of patients in hospital on August 17-21 METEOROLOGY.—The mean temperature was 59.2 Fah., and therefore 1.1 degrees higher than the preceding week. The maximum temperature 68 F., was recorded on the 12th inst., and the raimimum temperature 47 F., on the 15th inst. The Rainfall measured .67 inch. The general direc- tion was S., the reading of ,the Anemometer denoting 625 miles. JOHN H. GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.

THE "PECULIAR PEOPLE." ANOTHER DEATH. The Coroner for Southminster (Essex) had reported to him on Tuesday the death of another child of "Peculiar" parente,who succumbed to an attack of diphtheria. The father is a labourer at Tillingham, and on discovering that his child had contracted the disease he summoned the elder of the Church, who performed the ceremony of anointing with oil and " laying on hands." After linger- log for two days the child died. Another member of the family is suffering from the malady, but has not been attended by a doctor. Pending the result of the prow- -1 cutiou recently made by the Secretary of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children under similar cir- cumstances, the police will take no action.
